#3001. The Best Dividend Stock to Invest $1,000 in Right Now

Invitation Homes has a 4.5% dividend yield -- and it's growing.

I'm familiar with lots of great dividend-paying stocks, and I own a bunch of them, too. Every now and then I stumble upon one that's new to me, and that happened recently with Invitation Homes (INVH +1.32%). It's moving to the top of my watch list, as I wait to have $1,000 or more available to invest in more stock.

I'm super intrigued by the stock because:

That dividend is significant in part because Invitation Homes is a real estate investment trust (REIT) -- a company that owns lots of real estate, leasing it to tenants. REITs are required to pay out most of their earnings to shareholders, in the form of dividends.

While some REITs focus on retail buildings and others focus on warehouses, medical facilities, or other niche(s), Invitation Homes specializes in renting out homes to families and individuals. The company has noted that the average resident age is 39 and 54% have at least one child at home, as of 2025.

The company is growing well, with its third quarter featuring total revenue up 6.3% to $731 million. Its occupancy rate for homes it has rented out for a year or longer was 96.4%. It sold 658 wholly owned homes, bought 216 more, and used the proceeds from sales to fund share buybacks -- another way companies can reward shareholders. (When buybacks are factored in, the stock's total yield is 4.85%.)

Those kinds of yields are quite attractive, and the fact that payouts are growing is even better. The company's current quarterly payout per share is $0.30, and that's up from $0.22 in 2022 and $0.15 in 2020.

Invitation Homes' business prospects appear solid, in part because interest rates are relatively high these days, putting a damper on homebuyers' hopes and leaving more as renters. Housing shortages, meanwhile, have kept many homes priced high.

#2999. What Happens When Electronics Face The Real World? Precision Timing Holds The Key

Markus Lutz is Co-founder of SiTime Corporation. He is a MEMS expert, a prolific entrepreneur and inventor who holds over 100 patents.

​A decade ago, 25,000 white LED lights transformed the San Francisco–Oakland Bay Bridge into a living sculpture, illuminating the span with a shimmering light show that became a fixture of the skyline. Created by artist Leo Villareal and installed by the nonprofit organization Illuminate, The Bay Lights became a symbol of innovation, creativity and civic pride.

But in early 2023, the lights went dark. The cause? Electronics failure. The LEDs and the systems running them couldn’t withstand the harsh environmental conditions, constant vibration from traffic, extreme coastal weather and salt air corrosion. The lights failed one by one, until the entire system had to be shut down. Illuminate began a campaign to raise millions for a more resilient replacement. After nearly two years, a new installation is now complete. The 50,000 custom-engineered LEDs and the systems driving them are designed to endure the bridge’s harsh conditions through 2035 and beyond.

While the Bay Lights were an art installation, the conditions that contributed to their failure point to a much larger engineering reality. Reliability is not just determined by whether a device works on the bench. It depends on how that device behaves over years of exposure to temperature swings, vibration, humidity, airflow, mechanical stress and corrosion. The question is no longer simply “Does this component meet the spec?” It is “Will this system still perform when the environment is working against it?”

The future of electronics is increasingly being defined at the edge, in places like remote communication towers, under the hoods of electric vehicles, inside desert-based solar installations and on outdoor network infrastructure in extreme climates. In these environments, electronics must endure intense stressors, such as rapid temperature changes, vibration, shock, humidity, condensation, airflow, chemicals and mechanical strain.

Historically, design teams could protect vulnerable electronics with larger enclosures, added shielding, extra redundancy or wider design margins. Now, systems are getting smaller, lighter and more power-conscious, while environments are getting harsher. Resilience now must be designed into the architecture.

The question is whether it remains stable across the full range of conditions the product will face over its lifetime. Engineers should pressure-test five criteria early in the design process: environmental tolerance, stability over temperature, power consumption, size and integration and lifecycle reliability.

Timing components are one example of critical components that must withstand environmental stressors. Timing components provide a foundational reference for synchronizing data transmission, coordinating system operations and keeping interconnected components operating as intended.

In many electronic systems, timing is foundational to reliable operation. When timing falters, the consequences can cascade across an entire system, resulting in data corruption, communication failures or complete operational breakdown, making the integrity of the timing signal not merely a performance consideration.

Timing components that perform well in a lab may behave differently when exposed to airflow, vibration, board stress or thermal cycling. For systems deployed in vehicles, outdoor infrastructure, industrial equipment or data centers, the evaluation process must reflect the operating environment.

At the system level, component selection also involves trade-offs beyond basic timing accuracy. Engineers may need to consider how much flexibility they require across product configurations, how they will detect performance degradation in the field, how much board space the component can occupy and what its power requirements mean for thermal management or maintenance. These considerations can determine whether a timing component remains a discrete specification or becomes part of the broader system architecture.

Precision timing has always been a foundational layer of electronics responsible for syncing data, coordinating processors and keeping networks running smoothly. But when that timing is also rugged and resilient, it unlocks a new class of applications.

In smart infrastructure, for instance, electronics deployed in bridges or traffic systems need to function flawlessly for years despite environmental exposure. In vehicles, advanced driver-assistance systems and EV drivetrains require split-second coordination between sensors and actuators, often amid heat and road vibration. In aerospace and defense, systems in orbit or on the battlefield must perform across radical temperature fluctuations and mechanical stress. In emerging applications such as robotics and drones, timing components must also maintain accuracy despite constant motion, vibration and shock. In all of these cases, the reliability of the timing component is mission-critical.​

Engineers naturally focus on faster processors, better connectivity and smaller form factors in electronic systems. But none of those matter if the system cannot survive its environment.

Resilience by design requires a shift in process. Environmental assumptions should be defined at the beginning of development, not after failures appear in testing. Timing, power and thermal behavior should be evaluated together because they influence one another under stress. And qualification should account for the full lifecycle of the product, including vibration, temperature cycling, airflow, aging and maintenance constraints.

The story of the Bay Bridge art display provides a good analogy—a dazzling display that went dark because the underlying electronics couldn’t withstand the real world. But the revamped art display, now complete with a doubling of the number of LEDs and hardened components engineered to withstand wind, fog and vibration, is expected to last at least until 2035. It’s a reminder that resilience must be designed in from the start.

In much the same way, the electronics industry is rethinking what reliability really means. Components must hold performance through temperature, vibration, airflow, humidity, shock and time. The next generation of electronics will be defined by how fast it computes, how much data it moves and how consistently it operates when the world around it is anything but controlled.

#2998. At Its Current Rate, AGNC's Dividend Pays Back Today's Stock Price in About 6 Years. Will It Last That Long?

This mortgage REIT is currently yielding a jaw-dropping 17.5%. There's no free lunch.

The star attraction of AGNC (AGNC -0.67%) is, understandably, its dividend. The popular mortgage real estate investment trust (REIT) distributes $0.12 per share in cash to its shareholders every month. Over the course of the year, that translates to a current yield of 17.5%.

That's a lot, but you should approach any double-digit yield with a fair amount of skepticism. The allure of making back your initial investment in less than six years -- 5.7 years, to be exact -- is real. The risks are just as gargantuan.

AGNC has consistently shelled out $0.12 per share since April 2020. That's 79 consecutive distributions. It would take just 69 months of that payout to make back your initial investment. It seems like easy money, but it's not.

A month before the first of those 79 consecutive payments, the REIT's per-share dividend was paying $0.16. It was $0.18 per share a year before that. Keep the time machine running, and you will unearth a couple more dividend cuts. The monthly rate has been cut nearly in half over the past dozen years.

Investing in mortgage REITs is risky. The actual Agency-backed mortgage securities may seem safe, but the spread between the interest it pays on its funding and the income it collects from the securities is far from stable. Throw in AGNC's leveraged operations in a climate of rising rates, and things start to get a lot more unpredictable.

Don't be fooled by today's stock price. You see the $8.23 close heading into this week of trading. Work the math, and AGNC has paid out $9.48 per share over the past 79 months at the same distribution rate. The problem is that at the start of this run, the stock was at $12.74 before the first of those reduced monthly disbursements. Its book value is deteriorating. The yield appears fantastic, but be aware of the risks.

#2995. Top Wall Street analysts are upbeat about the prospects for these 3 stocks

September ended on a mixed note, with the S&P 500 and Dow Jones Industrial Average falling 0.5% and 4.3%, respectively, while the Nasdaq Composite climbed 1.9%. Markets remained on edge with higher oil prices and Treasury yields weighing on investor sentiment and triggering fears of additional interest rate hikes.

In this climate, investors with a long-term horizon can track recommendations from top Wall Street analysts to find stocks with solid growth prospects.

Here are three stocks favored by some of Wall Street's top pros, according to TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Neocloud company CoreWeave (CRWV) is benefiting from robust AI-driven demand for cloud computing.

Recently, JPMorgan analyst Samik Chatterjee upgraded CoreWeave to buy from hold, raising his price target to $125 from $120. The analyst cited favorable pricing, driven by solid demand, for the upgrade.

Chatterjee said CoreWeave is now more willing to sign premium priced, shorter-term contracts, which lift his revenue and margin estimates. CRWV stock performance has been underwhelming as investors worry about high capital spending, the analyst noted. That said, he believes that higher prices and increased margins will more than offset the rise in debt to fund capital spending, improving the stock's valuation.

Chatterjee noted the 25% price hike in July across CoreWeave's product offerings and frequent price increases by rivals like Nebius (NBIS), adding that some peers are charging nearly three times more for short-term compute contracts than what CoreWeave charges on longer-term deals.

"CoreWeave's leverage of the opportunity [is] evident in its recent press release highlighting contracts signed in F3Q at ~$40M/MW," said Chatterjee. JPMorgan said higher pricing is already contributing 5-10 percentage points to contract margins versus earlier deals.

Chatterjee ranks No. 4 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 75% of the time, delivering an average return of 55.3%. See CoreWeave Statistics on TipRanks.

Palo Alto Networks (PANW) is a provider of cybersecurity solutions across network, cloud, security operations, AI and identity. Following a discussion with management, BTIG analyst Gray Powell reiterated a buy rating on PANW and raised his price target to $425 from $404.

After a review of Palo Alto's product-level growth catalysts, Powell sees clear upside to both revenue and next-generation security (NGS) annual recurring revenue (ARR) in fiscal 2027. He expects PANW to deliver pro forma revenue growth of more than 17%, above the consensus estimate of 14.6%, and NGS ARR growth of more than 26% versus the Street's expectation of 24.5%.

Over the past five years, the Palo Alto business mix, has moved to higher-growth product areas and new markets. from what was earlier viewed as a hardware-centric network security company, Powell said. The 5-star analyst estimates that hardware firewalls and attached subscriptions now account for less than 50% of revenue.

Meanwhile, high-growth solutions, including Prisma SASE, software firewalls, Cortex XSIAM, Chronosphere and Idira now represent more than 35% of PANW's revenue. "Investors may not fully realize the growth potential within PANW's platform story," Powell said.

BTIG is optimistic about the prospects of Chronosphere in observability, CyberArk in identity and software firewalls and Prisma AIRS in the network and AI security business.

Powell ranks No. 320 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 61% of the time, delivering an average return of 17.3%. See Palo Alto Networks KPIs on TipRanks.

In a research note on September 30, Rosenblatt analyst Scott Devitt reiterated a buy rating on e-commerce and cloud computing platform Amazon (AMZN), raising his price target to $360 from $335. Fears that agentic commerce poses a major threat to Amazon's advertising business seem "overstated," he said.

Devitt noted that the growing adoption of personal agents and AI shopping assistants has sparked concern about displacement of Amazon's retail media business. But the 5-star analyst remains bullish, given Amazon's ability to adapt to changing consumer behavior, as it's done in the past.

Additionally, Devitt argues that even if an AI agent helps a shopper discover a product, Amazon can still handle the purchase and delivery, keeping much of the transaction value. He highlighted that management believes that existing Amazon shoppers will likely continue to start their shopping journey on the platform because it has better personalization, accurate pricing and inventory information that third-party agents still lack.

Overall, Devitt expects Amazon's retail business to prove a net beneficiary of agentic commerce. Fewer clicks would not hurt the company if the value of each remaining click and overall volumes keep rising, he said. The argument is that even if Amazon loses some sponsored ad revenue over the next few years, it would likely be only a small part of the company's total business and could be offset by growth in other areas.

"Consistent with our AI framework, we continue to view Amazon as one of the most resilient leaders in our coverage, combining owned compute infrastructure with a physical fulfillment moat," said Devitt. His bullish view is supported, Devitt said, by expectations that Amazon Web Services (AWS) will exit the year at a more than 45% growth rate with continued margin improvement.

Devitt ranks No. 765 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 49% of the time, delivering an average return of 12%. See Amazon Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

#2993. Donnarumma, errore in Italia-Francia: critiche, ma resta capitano

Il portiere ha rimediato con le parate su Rabiot e Cherki; sui social torna il confronto con Carnesecchi e Vicario.

L’errore di Gianluigi Donnarumma sullo 0-1 della Francia ha riacceso le critiche dopo la vittoria dell’Italia. Secondo quanto riportato da SportMediaset, Il capitano azzurro ha poi risposto con diverse parate, ma giornali e social sono tornati a discutere le sue prestazioni. Nella Nazionale di Roberto Mancini, però, il suo posto non è in discussione.

CLICCA QUI E SEGUI LAZIOPRESS.IT ANCHE SU INSTAGRAM

Nella vittoria contro la Francia, i due episodi negativi per gli azzurri sono arrivati alle estremità del campo. Oltre all’errore di Donnarumma, Sebastiano Esposito ha sbagliato un rigore sull’1-1. La giocata del portiere ha avuto maggiore risonanza anche per la sua esperienza, la sua statura nel gruppo e la fascia da capitano.

Donnarumma ha concesso l’occasione a Olise, ma è intervenuto più volte nel corso della partita: tra le parate ricordate ci sono quelle su Rabiot e Cherki. Gli interventi hanno attenuato solo in parte il giudizio sull’errore. I giornali lo hanno bocciato e sui social sono riprese le prese in giro nei suoi confronti, riferisce SportMediaset.

Alle reazioni si sono aggiunte quelle dei tifosi inglesi, che hanno richiamato un dato sul Manchester City: la squadra ha subito almeno un gol in ciascuna delle ultime otto partite.

Le critiche accompagnano Donnarumma anche nel rapporto con una parte dei tifosi, non soltanto quelli del Milan. Da quando ha lasciato l’Italia per giocare all’estero, il suo gradimento non è cresciuto. Il talento del portiere classe 1999 resta riconosciuto, ma a 27 anni alle grandi parate continuano ad alternarsi errori, soprattutto nelle uscite alte e quando deve gestire il pallone con i piedi.

Nel confronto sui portieri azzurri c’è chi sostiene la candidatura di Carnesecchi, che anche quest’anno si sta confermando ad alti livelli. Il terzo portiere della Nazionale è Vicario: tornato in Serie A in estate, sta dando sicurezza alla difesa della Juventus. Nell’Under 21 c’è Palmisani.

La gestione del pallone con i piedi rimane un aspetto importante nel calcio moderno e nel gioco di Mancini. È su questo punto che l’errore contro la Francia ha riportato l’attenzione, pur senza cancellare il valore di Donnarumma tra i pali.

#2991. If Staying in Nvidia Stock Scares You, You Might Want To Consider This ETF

Nvidia's troubles may suggest that ETF investing is more suitable for some investors.

The controversy surrounding Nvidia’s leadership is undoubtedly a sucker punch to many investors. Its dominance in AI chips and rapid growth made it appear unstoppable, attracting numerous investors. Now, these same investors may have become more concerned about the stock as scandals have ended co-founder and CEO Jensen Huang's leadership.

However, AI is likely here to stay, a factor that may leave scared investors reluctant to stop investing in chip stocks altogether. Fortunately, the ETF world offers a solution that minimizes exposure to Nvidia while allowing investors to still profit from the AI trade.

Instead of Nvidia, risk-averse investors can turn to the iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX -4.19%). SOXX is a semiconductor industry ETF that invests in 30 stocks across the sector, offering easy diversification. Moreover, it caps the weight of any one stock in the fund at 8%.

Consequently, its Nvidia exposure is only around 7.1%. For those concerned about Nvidia, this may compare favorably to its most prominent competitor, the VanEck Semiconductor ETF (SMH -3.19%). The VanEck ETF sets its cap at 20% and, as a result, holds about 18% in Nvidia.

Additionally, Nvidia is the third-largest holding in SOXX, not the first. Instead, it holds larger stakes in both AMD and Broadcom, with AMD accounting for 10.1% of its holdings and Broadcom making up about 8.3%. That approach arguably makes SOXX a trade-off between tactical concentration and low-cost breadth.

Furthermore, SOXX achieves this with minimal effect on returns. Over the last 10 years, average total returns exceed 32% annually. In comparison, the iShares Core S&P 500 ETF (IVV -0.76%) averaged 14.9% per year over the same period, indicating that SOXX typically outperforms the market.

Also, if measured since the fund's inception in 2001, total returns are almost 15% yearly. Since that includes the 2008-2009 financial crisis, it suggests to investors that they can still earn positive returns over the long term no matter what happens in the market.

To achieve such gains, investors pay an annual management fee of 0.33%, which is low or on par with other semiconductor-focused ETFs. That means for every $10,000 investment, shareholders pay the fund only $33 in fees. That makes it an economical way to safely benefit from the growth of the chip industry with far less risk and with far less time commitment than is required to properly manage individual stocks.

Given the fund's track record and comparatively low fees, SOXX may be an excellent choice for chip-stock investors who have been burned by Nvidia's recent price action.

Indeed, owning a stock like Nvidia carries both reward and risk, and some investors may feel that the growth potential is worth the risk of holding an individual stock.

However, for investors with less risk tolerance, SOXX offers the potential for market-beating returns with considerably less direct exposure to a stock like Nvidia. When also considering the low management fees, such an investment may be a more suitable choice for many chip stock investors.

#2990. Streaming’s Missed Opportunity: Being A Fan Is Getting More Expensive

Wayne Lonstein, CEO, VFT Solutions, Inc. Anti-Piracy, Social Media and Cybersecurity law and practice.

​In my last Forbes article, I argued that streaming was recreating the cable model consumers thought they had escaped: multiple subscriptions, rising prices and fragmented viewing.

Professional sports may be the clearest example of that missed opportunity. Leagues and franchises are enjoying extraordinary financial success. Broadcasters and technology companies are investing billions in live sports, while premium seating, sponsorships and exclusive experiences are generating new revenue.

For consumers, following a favorite team, whether from the couch or the stadium, has become increasingly expensive and complicated. The sports industry has mastered the art of monetizing fans. But has it done enough to improve their experience?

According to Houlihan Lokey’s recent research, the NFL’s annualized national media rights value rose from approximately $6.45 billion under its previous agreement to $12.25 billion under its current arrangement, an increase of roughly 90%. The NBA’s comparable figure climbed from $2.66 billion to $6.9 billion annually, a 159% increase.

Major League Baseball’s national rights rose from approximately $1.55 billion to $1.88 billion annually under its original 2022 to 2028 agreements, an increase of roughly 22%. MLB subsequently restructured portions of those agreements, announcing new partnerships with ESPN, NBCUniversal and Netflix for 2026 through 2028.

Although these comparisons span different contract periods, they reveal intense competition for programming that audiences still watch live. Sports delivers passionate engagement and the simultaneous viewership advertisers prize. But the commercial success of sports has not necessarily translated into a better viewing experience.

Streaming promised greater convenience, flexibility and control. Instead, fans increasingly need a subscription strategy just to follow their teams.

Consider the NFL. According to YouTube’s Sunday Ticket information, the service provides out-of-market Sunday afternoon games but excludes other broadcasts and streaming exclusives. The NBA’s standard League Pass charges viewers per season, but local and nationally televised games face restrictions. Watching those matchups may require ESPN, Peacock, Prime Video or another provider. MLB.TV lists its 2026 full-season package at one cost, yet local and nationally exclusive games may require additional access.

These are different products, not complete viewing packages. None guarantees every live game nationwide. Imagine paying for a league’s streaming service, only to discover that tonight’s game is somewhere else. That is not the simplicity streaming promised.

The financial pressure extends beyond the screen.

According to Gametime resale ticket data reported by Axios in February 2026, average NFL resale ticket prices increased 173% between 2015 and 2025 after adjusting for inflation. MLB resale prices rose 111%, while NBA resale prices climbed 70%.

These figures reflect one resale marketplace, not league-wide face-value averages. Nevertheless, they illustrate the growing expense confronting many fans. Add parking, transportation, concessions and merchandise, and attending a game can become a major family expenditure.

Higher media rights fees do not necessarily cause higher ticket prices. Demand, limited seating, premium experiences and resale dynamics all influence what consumers pay.

The pattern also extends beyond sports. Concerts, attractions and other live experiences face similar pressures, contributing to what has become known as “funflation.” The common thread is unmistakable: Entertainment businesses are finding new ways to capture value, while consumers are paying more to participate.

Premium seating, exclusive access and luxury experiences can be enormously profitable. But the sports industry possesses an asset that cannot simply be purchased: generational loyalty.

Fans inherit allegiances through families, neighborhoods and shared traditions. Those emotional connections help make franchises, sponsorships and broadcast rights so valuable. The danger is not that every fan will stop watching or attending. It is that participation gradually becomes a privilege rather than a shared experience. A family priced out of attending games may still watch on television. But what happens when television access becomes expensive and confusing, too?

There are signs of progress. The NBA’s latest media agreements significantly expand regular-season games on free, over-the-air television, demonstrating that commercial growth and accessibility can coexist.​

The solution is not to eliminate premium content or expect media rights fees to decline. It is to make the fan experience as important as the next rights negotiation.

Consumers need simpler subscriptions, transparent pricing, easier game discovery, flexible viewing options and meaningful free access. Technology should remove friction, not create another layer of it. The next chapter of streaming should be judged by more than what companies pay to acquire content. It should be judged by how easily consumers can find, access and afford what they love.

Leagues and franchises have never been richer. The real test of their success is whether the fans who built that value can still afford to be part of it.

What if consumers could spend less time managing subscriptions and more time enjoying entertainment?

In a future article, I’ll explore a concept currently being developed to simplify how consumers discover, access and pay for streaming content. The approach brings together content discovery, subscription management and personalized viewing choices, with the goal of giving consumers greater convenience, flexibility and control over what they watch and spend.

Streaming changed how content is delivered. The next opportunity is to transform how consumers access it.​​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#2984. Spruce Power: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Accounting gains lifted Spruce's GAAP net income, but management says that jump is not recurring operating cash, so profits alone can mislead.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 12, 2026, Spruce Power (SPRU -1.41%) announced a nonbinding term sheet to extend its debt maturities. Clear debt resolution matters far more than temporary weather slowdowns for long-term investors. Three things management said stand out.

Heavy project debt creates ongoing default risks, making capital restructuring the top operational priority. Securing a preliminary agreement offers breathing room to negotiate final terms and potentially remove going-concern disclosures.

Overhead expenses threaten cash flow when power generation drops due to bad weather. Management cut corporate administrative costs by modernizing internal workflows and leaning into automated software tools.

Reported profits can deceive investors when mark-to-market derivative gains swing earnings without bringing in fresh cash. Accounting adjustments drove the quarterly bottom-line jump rather than expanding operating collections.

Operating expenses are moving lower as automated workflows replace manual tasks across administrative divisions. A base of roughly 83,000 residential solar contracts provides steady recurring cash flow, while management focuses on disciplined expense control and capital allocation.

Finalizing debt agreements remains the critical bridge toward expanding capital-light servicing partnerships and driving long-term enterprise value.

#2981. Accelevation: 3 Key Takeaways for Long-Term Investors

Its customer base is expanding beyond Texas, but the company's ability to keep execution tight as factory capacity ramps up remains an open question.

On its third-quarter 2026 earnings call, held on Nov. 13, 2026, Accelevation (ACCV -6.50%) announced a 66% increase in third-quarter revenue to $202 million as demand for data center hardware accelerated. Data center operators are racing to bring compute power online, driving sales of custom electrical and thermal equipment. The business expanded its production footprint while building a $1.7 billion backlog, or orders booked but not yet fulfilled, providing clear visibility into future sales. Three takeaways from the call show how the company is scaling its operations.

Management shifted manufacturing away from traditional on-site assembly toward factory-built modular systems that bundle power distribution, airflow containment, and structural systems. Integrating these components into a single shipment expands content per installed megawatt and increases revenue per customer project.

The company added seven major customer accounts in the third quarter, reducing reliance on top clients. Expansion into new states such as Indiana and North Dakota broadened the geographic reach beyond historical hubs like Texas, creating a more diversified revenue base.

Data center customers prioritize execution speed because delays postpone revenue generation on hardware investments. Owning the process from engineering design through installation allows the business to deliver rapid turnarounds in the white space, the server hall equipment area that supports live computing.

Production momentum is accelerating as data center builders rush to shorten construction schedules, and management is expanding manufacturing space to meet incoming volume. A U.S.-centric supply chain and tight control over capital expenditures, or physical facility spending, keep fixed costs low while higher volume, pricing, and project mix expand gross margin, the percentage of revenue left after direct production costs. Leadership is closely monitoring project execution and manufacturing utilization as factory capacity ramps up.

Expanding customer contracts and factory capacity position the platform to convert recorded orders into long-term cash flow.

#2979. Three Questions (And Answers) To Secure The AI-Enabled Workplace

Pieter Danhieux is the Co-Founder and Chairman/CEO of Secure Code Warrior.

​If any workplace maxim has proven true in the 21st century, it’s that every employee is a technologist.​

Today’s professionals are, after all, universally reliant on devices, software, the internet and cloud services to manage timesheets, research marketing reports, submit budgets, diagnose operational issues and pursue innovation. The fact that all of this has grown so commonplace illustrates how technology is increasingly demystified and democratized. Once considered fairly esoteric—unknowable, save for those on IT teams—these advancements are now easy to instantly obtain and consume for those in any department.​

So, it should come as no surprise that these professionals are deploying AI just like any other technology: Most senior business and technology leaders say AI is embedded into core functions and “deployed broadly” across the enterprise. Four of five expect AI agents to make key, impactful decisions for their organization within the next year. Three of five say their company has redesigned job roles to prepare specifically for an AI-enabled workplace.​

But there remains a critical—and relatively unaddressed—issue: AI adoption is expanding faster than AI readiness. Less than one-quarter of those senior business and technology leaders believe their staffers are fully ready for AI. Nearly four of five agree that the speed of AI will outpace their organization’s governance models.

For certain, employees will always be capable of harnessing AI to boost productivity. But they often do so without an understanding of the security implications of connecting tools, granting permissions, sharing information or allowing an AI agent to act on their behalf.​ In response, security leaders need to find out who’s using AI agents, what data and access employees are allowing for them, what the agents are empowered to do and whether teams are able to recognize when an AI-powered workflow emerges as unsafe.​

To arrive at this level of heightened awareness, these leaders must address the following three questions:​

​This is a complex line of pursuit because adoption is so decentralized, with “shadow AI” highly pervasive. According to results from a 2026 PagerDuty survey, two-thirds of office professionals say they’ve used AI tools or services on the job, even though they realized the activity wasn’t permitted by their company’s policies.

​As a result, shadow AI becomes an organizational problem. Employees can move from chatting with an AI assistant to building workflows and automations without ever thinking of themselves as AI developers. Banning these tools rarely works because employees adopt them for a simple reason: They make work easier.

Instead, security and business leaders should pair visibility and governance with AI literacy, so employees have the skills to make safe decisions themselves. Part of that literacy is understanding that LLMs and other AI assistants can’t be trusted to produce secure code on their own. Without training in what to look for and how these tools go wrong, even simple automations and coding projects can introduce unacceptable risk.

​Most employees would never deliberately share confidential company information with an unknown third party. Yet, many don’t think twice about doing so with an AI assistant.

​In fact, according to the same PagerDuty survey, “88% of office professionals have shared work-related information with public AI tools such as ChatGPT.” Additionally, “43% have shared emails or other correspondence, 40% have shared meeting notes or summaries, 34% have input customer data or information and 31% have shared financial information or confidential company documents and strategies.”​

​Acceptable-use policies are necessary, but they can’t fix this on their own. The goal is behavior change, not just awareness. Employees need practical judgment and responsible habits that shape what they actually do with AI, not just what they know about it. Before connections are made, staffers should ask, “Does AI really need this access? What can it do with the permissions I’m granting? What could happen if it behaves unexpectedly?” In the process, they’ll gain an understanding of and appreciation for zero-trust principles that enforce least privilege.​

​Ultimately, it’s about accountability. Policies are meaningless without workforce buy-in.

​Governance should go beyond “rules” and technical controls to ensure teams possess the skills and mindsets required to use AI responsibly whenever they share data, connect a system, grant access or automate an action. But compliance shouldn’t become the end goal. The more important goal is to ensure training changes what an employee does at the moment they share data, connect a system, grant access or automate an action—for example, if they immediately detect an unsafe automation or permission before it emerges as a security incident.

​Your employees are already using AI. The only question is whether they’ve been trained to use it well. Ultimately, employees must understand that giving an AI agent access is a risk decision, even if it doesn’t seem like one.

​Toward this goal, security leaders should pair technical governance with workforce capabilities, with insights about adoption, guardrails and practical education that enable employees to pursue all of the advantages of AI innovation while still operating safely.

​Restrictions alone can’t do this—not when everybody is a technologist who can get around the rules if they feel it’s necessary. However, when these same employees not only know the guardrails but why they matter, they benefit their organizations by leveraging AI advancements without unduly increasing risk.​

Forbes Technology Council is an invitation-only community for world-class CIOs, CTOs and technology executives. Do I qualify?

#2977. Is Plug Power Stock a Millionaire Maker or a Money Pit?

Down more than 70% from its 52-week high, could Plug Power stock be an explosive winner?

Despite some brief surges of bullish momentum, it's been a tough year for Plug Power (PLUG -0.45%) shareholders. The hydrogen technologies specialist's stock has fallen roughly 51% over the last 12 months, and it trades down 73% from its 52-week high.

While the company's valuation has plummeted across the stretch, there have been some meaningfully positive developments for the business -- including a return to double-digit sales growth and a substantial improvement for its gross margin. On the heels of a huge valuation pullback, is Plug Power a potential millionaire-maker stock or a money pit that will keep getting deeper?

Plug Power's revenue increased 13% year over year in the third quarter to reach roughly $201 million, and its gross margin for the period came in at -3% -- improving from -68% in the prior-year period. While the company recorded a breakeven quarter thanks to mark-to-market accounting on its investments, the company's unrestricted cash pile declined by $28 million to $134 million.

While the business has seen a substantial improvement for its gross margin, it's still posting sizable operating losses and will likely need to raise new funds in the near future. If the company moves to sell new stock in order to raise capital, its share price will probably see a significant pullback in response to the dilutive impact of new shares being issued. With the stock already trading dangerously close to the $1 per share level needed to remain listed on the Nasdaq exchange, that sets up a dangerous valuation dynamic.

With that in mind, I think it's likely that things will get worse for Plug Power stock before they have the chance to get better -- and the chances of big losses are significantly greater than the chances of big returns. On the other hand, the stock does have the potential to deliver explosive returns if Plug Power can deliver results that show its business is financially viable over the long term.

#2976. Have Semiconductors Bottomed? Here's How the Current Chip Stock Plunge Compares With Past Bear Markets.

The current chip pullback is near where others have bottomed… with one notable exception.

After reaching all-time highs in June, the broader semiconductor sector has gone into a severe bear market.

The first big pullback came in July, with the release of powerful Chinese open-weight models and notable short-sellers making valuation-based calls. In October and into November, fears of a Democratic sweep of both houses of Congress and state governorships fueled concerns about a data center ban or slowdown.

The semiconductor sector has been the best-performing sector over the long term, but it's also the most volatile. So if chip stocks have bottomed, now could be a great entry point for long-term investors. But has the pullback ended? Here's how the current drawdown compares with past chip bear markets.

The chip sector, as denoted by the iShares Semiconductor ETF (SOXX -4.19%), fell 39.5% from its June high to its recent low on Nov. 5. That is very severe, but roughly in line with the ETF's periodic declines over the past 10 years. In fact, the current 2026 sell-off puts it right in the middle of the average chip sector sell-off over the past decade.

As you can see, the current drawdown is right in the middle in terms of magnitude compared with the other four major drawdowns in the SOXX over the past 10 years.

The reasons for these past drawdowns were also somewhat similar to the current chip plunge. Like 2018, this year is a midterm election year during a Trump administration. Also, as in 2018, inflation had begun to tick up -- albeit off very low levels -- and the Federal Reserve was beginning to raise interest rates, which hadn't really happened since the global financial crisis of 2008.

The same sort of thing happened in late 2021, only to a greater degree. The post-pandemic shortages and stimulus spurred a wave of inflation not seen since the 1980s, prompting the Federal Reserve to raise interest rates at the fastest pace in its history. Rising inflation and interest rates in both cases caused companies and consumers to pull back on technology spending, which had been pulled forward during the pandemic. They had also been strong during the cloud computing boom in 2017-2018.

In 2024 and into 2025, government policy, combined with overly optimistic valuations at the beginning of that year driven by the AI boom, caused the pullback. The Biden administration tightened export controls on China during that period, while companies largely deferred IT spending on PCs and traditional servers to make room to buy expensive Nvidia (NVDA -1.06%) GPUs. That sell-off continued into the early second Trump term, when tariff worries, culminating in "Liberation Day," caused a final market flush and a market bottom.

Today, we are seeing a mix of all the different factors that colored prior sell-offs, aside from the unique COVID-19 crash. In these three other scenarios, a mix of high valuations, inflation, rising interest rates, and restrictive government policy led investors to question the high growth expectations embedded in chip stock valuations.

Of course, these four other examples only encompass recent history. Some investors compare the current AI pullback to the bust after the dot-com boom.

If the AI boom were to come undone similarly, the consequences would be more severe than even the severe pullback we've already seen. In the case of the SOXX, the semiconductor index never even recovered its prior high before a second crisis -- the housing bust of 2008 -- took it down even further than the dot-com crash.

Most know that the dot-com boom peaked in early 2000, which was actually before the SOXX ETF started trading in July 2001. However, after peaking in early August 2001, the SOXX eventually retreated 70.2% to its bottom in November 2008. The ETF didn't recover its August 2001 high until January 2014 -- 12.5 years after its prior peak!

If, in fact, the AI boom is one large "overbuild," as the dot-com build-out was, then this sell-off might not be over yet.

In this investor's opinion, the current situation seems more akin to more recent semiconductor bear markets than the dot-com bust. As Blackstone (BX -0.52%) CEO Jon Gray said in a recent interview with Bloomberg News:

If the current pullback is indeed a growth and supply-related scare, rather than a demand bubble bursting, then the chip sector has just about fallen in line with recent bear-market bottoms in semiconductors. That's why I have been buying more chip stocks over the past two weeks, and I would recommend that others with a long-term investing horizon do the same.

#2974. Junk bonds are 'flashing yellow.' Watch these warning signs

Cracks are forming in the junk bond market as investors demand higher payouts for owning the market's riskiest debt. It isn't time to ditch high-yield bonds, but investors should pay attention to the warning signs.

High-yield bonds now yield 8.1%, up from 7.22% a month ago. The increase reflects a jump in yields across the curve as investors bake in more inflation from high energy prices and other pressures, including concern about the deficit — hitting nearly $2 trillion in the fiscal year that ended Sept. 30.

The high-yield market is also showing stress on the credit side with spreads recently widening to levels not seen since April, according to the Federal Reserve Bank of St. Louis. Credit spreads are the difference in yield between the bonds and Treasurys of similar maturities. Wider spreads mean investors are demanding higher yields for holding corporate debt, viewing it as riskier.

Spreads are at 315 basis points in the overall high-yield market, higher than a year ago but still below levels in March when they reached 346 bps. One basis point equals one one-hundredth of a percent, or 0.01%.

The high-yield market consists of bonds rated BB+ by S&P and Fitch and Ba1 and under by Moody's. The lowest-rated cohort, CCC and below, has seen the most movement with spreads climbing dramatically over the past year to roughly 1,250 bps.

Right now, the high-yield market is "flashing yellow" but is "far from red," said Michael Arone, chief investment strategist at State Street Investment Management.

It makes sense that investors are demanding more compensation for taking on additional credit risk as borrowing costs rise, he said.

Yields are elevated across the board with the 10-year Treasury reaching its highest level since 2002 earlier in the week.

"The bigger question is whether this is simply a repricing of interest rate risk, or the beginning of a more fundamental reassessment of credit quality," Arone said.

He's in a wait-and-see mode since earnings are still growing, interest-coverage ratios remain good, and while default rates have ticked up some, he believes it is not concerning.

That said, the starting point in spreads is likely weighing on investors' psyche, since they are still low by historical standards.

"There's a small margin of error here, which I also think raises the anxiety level," Arone explained. "The compensation that investors are receiving for taking on this credit risk isn't overwhelming relative to history, and therefore subtle changes in credit spreads can be concerning."

While there may be concerns about part of the lower-rated market, the overall high-yield market is in good fundamental shape.

In fact, credit quality is at a record high, with BB bonds making up over 60% of the market compared with 38% prior to the global financial crisis, said Kelley Gerrity, a fixed income strategist at Morgan Stanley Investment Management.

"We've had higher-quality companies coming in, and with higher rates now, you also have more discipline from companies that are more indebted ... just because of the higher cost of capital, so that actually is creating a bit of a healthier picture as well," she said.

The lowest tier of high yield has always been more speculative, since the bonds have a higher default risk. Therefore, it's no surprise that they are the ones seeing the most widening in spreads, said Collin Martin, head of fixed income research and strategy at the Schwab Center for Financial Research.

The overall move in high-yield spreads have been "orderly," he said. "Are there cracks forming? I think the cracks are logical cracks with the lowest-rated bonds and it's too early to say that it's spreading to the broad credit market."

In addition, fluctuations within the CCC-rated and lower spreads have been idiosyncratic, Gerrity added. Morgan Stanley recently broke down the spreads in the lowest cohort into two buckets: performing assets and non-performing assets, which are defined as spreads above 1,000 bps.

The non-performing spread to worst is 2,818 bps, while the performing bucket — the biggest piece of the CCC market — is 461 bps, she said.

"It is isolated," she said. "I'm not necessarily sure that that will go away anytime soon, but it doesn't tell the story of overall concern and flashing caution for credit markets right now."

For now, investors should remain selective within higher yield, Gerrity advised.

"We're looking for the best relative value opportunities, and we think that exists within that single-B cohort, and so we're leaning into that," she said.

Investors should be concerned if there is a steep widening in the broader high-yield market.

But there's scant evidence of stress in the BB cohort. Spreads in that group are at 194 bps, up from 179 bps a year ago — although the move hasn't been in a straight line.

"We're going to be focusing our attention on what are considered some stronger businesses," Martin said. "If we start to see the markets demand higher spreads there also, that's what we'll be looking at to see if risks are really rising."

It's also important to understand that the Federal Reserve is raising rates in a strong economic environment, said R.J. Gallo, chief investment officer of global fixed income at Federated Hermes. The central bank is concerned about an inflation problem amid high fossil fuel prices due to the Iran war, while growth has been stronger than expected, he noted.

"With growth being good as the reason that the Fed is hiking, then you wouldn't expect high yield to blow out because growth means revenues stay up, means cash flow and profitability stays up," he explained.

High yield becomes a disaster when the economy is heading into a sharp economic downturn, Gallo said.

"That's when spreads really widen out," he said. "But a recession is not the odds-on bet. Now, talk to me in six months if the Fed keeps hiking over and over, if oil prices stay high for longer, well, then maybe we will start to wonder."

#2972. China’s slowdown deepens in October as industrial output, retail sales, investment miss estimates

China's economic slowdown worsened in October with retail sales, investment and industrial output all missing economists' estimates, as analysts warn a growing reliance on external demand leaves growth more exposed to shocks abroad.

Monday's data showed the investment slump deepened and retail sales again lagged industrial output, as consumer spending languished with the property market downturn eroding household wealth for a fifth year.

Retail sales, a gauge of consumption, rose 0.4% from a year ago in October, disappointing analysts' forecast for a 0.8% rise, slowing from a six-month high of 1.1% in September.

Industrial output expanded 4.8% from a year earlier last month, missing analysts' estimates for 5.3% growth and sharply slowing from 5.5% in September, the highest level in five months.

For the first ten months of this year, fixed-asset investments, including property and infrastructure, shrank 8.1%, deepening a collapse plaguing the economy this year that's unprecedented outside the Covid-19 period. That figure marked a significant deterioration compared to analysts' expectations for a 7% decline.

The economic malaise also weighed on the labor market. Unemployment inched lower to 5.1% in October, while the jobless rate for those aged between 16 and 24, excluding students, hovered near the highest level in a year, standing at 18% in September.

Growth in the world's second-largest economy bounced off three-year lows in the third quarter, expanding 4.5% from a year earlier. Goldman Sachs expects China's economy to slow to 4.6% this year, from 5% expansion in 2025, before easing further to 4.5% in 2027.

The Wall Street bank expects the low inflation to extend into next year, with consumer inflation ticking lower to 0.8% this year, 0.5 in 2027. It cited soft domestic demand, abundant aggregate supply, easing commodity-price momentum and potential artificial intelligence disruption risks.

Despite the moderation, the projected growth rate still puts the economy on track to achieve Beijing's ambition of doubling 2020 per capita income by 2035, Goldman Sachs said in a note Monday.

Subdued consumer inflation and roaring exports paint a picture of an economy increasingly dominant in global markets but struggling to generate prosperity at home. Consumer inflation ticked down to 0.7% in October, its first deceleration after two straight months of increases, while exports surged by a more-than-expected 35.2%.

China is on track to surpass last year's record $1.2 trillion trade surplus despite growing trade tensions, with the gap reaching the $1 trillion threshold by October — the fastest it has ever reached that mark.

The strong export momentum has largely been driven by global demand for AI-related technology and equipment. Any signs of pullback in the industry could weigh on China's exports, analysts warn.

"The market was already worried" about a slowdown in the AI capital-spending cycle even before the Nvidia scandal that put flows of restricted chips to China under renewed scrutiny, said Zhiwei Zhang, president and chief economist at Pinpoint Asset Management.

"Now the risk has become even more serious," Zhang said, forecasting further downside risk for global economic growth next year.

The increasingly lopsided growth came as major trading partners have ramped up calls for the economy to rebalance. Headwinds on China's export engine could intensify next year, with further trade restrictions from the European Union posing a critical downside risk, Citi Research analysts said.

"More EU trade measures targeting China look plausible to us, potentially adding to tensions ahead," the bank said, though Beijing is likely to be open to negotiating "reasonable, practical concessions," such as import pledges, sector-specific deals and value-added tax rebate cuts.

— CNBC's Evelyn Cheng contributed to this report.

#2971. 3 Things Everyone Should Know About the 2027 Social Security COLA

We're just a few weeks from a major change to Social Security.

We're just a few weeks away from the 2027 Social Security cost-of-living adjustment (COLA), which will provide much-needed financial assistance to seniors who have been struggling with rising costs throughout the year. The first payments with the new COLA won't arrive until January, but it isn't too early to start understanding how they will affect your budget.

Here are the three most important things to know about the 2027 Social Security COLA when preparing for next year.

The 2027 Social Security COLA came in at 3.6%. This is above average compared to the last 50 years of COLAs, and it's the largest COLA since 2023. However, it still might not be enough to cover all your rising costs.

Since the COLA is a percentage, everyone sees a slightly different dollar-value increase, depending on what their checks are now. Those who are currently receiving large benefits will see bigger increases from the COLA.

The typical retired worker received $2,092 per month from Social Security as of October 2026. A 3.6% COLA will add roughly $75 to this benefit amount. However, some of this will be offset by rising Medicare Part B premiums for those enrolled in this program. Spousal benefits will see a more modest average increase -- up to $1,024 per month from $988 per month in October 2026.

You should receive a personalized COLA notice from the Social Security Administration giving your exact benefit increase in early December. If you have any questions about how the COLA will affect your checks, wait until after you've received this notice to reach out to your local Social Security office to ask.

The Social Security Administration pays benefits on the second, third, or fourth Wednesdays of the month for most seniors. Your payment date depends on the day of the month in which you were born. The relevant dates for January 2027 are:

There are a few exceptions to this rule, though. If you're receiving Supplemental Security Income (SSI) or you began receiving Social Security prior to May 1997, you typically receive your Social Security checks on the 3rd of the month. However, since Jan. 3, 2027, is a Sunday, and the preceding Friday is a federal holiday, you'll receive these payments on Dec. 31, 2026.

#2969. Japan's economy unexpectedly contracts in third-quarter as elevated energy costs bite

Japan's economy unexpectedly contracted in the third quarter, shrinking at an annualized 0.1% compared to the previous quarter, as elevated energy prices weighed on consumption and net exports.

A Reuters poll of economists had estimated the economy to grow at 0.3% in the three months to September, while GDP had expanded at 1.4% in the previous quarter.

Japan's exports beat expectations for two of the three months in the quarter, but net exports weighed on the economy due to higher costs of energy imports, cutting 0.6 percentage point from the country's growth.

Energy subsidies for households had reportedly expired in September, exposing consumers to higher energy prices brought about by the Iran war.

The GDP data could complicate Japan's policy normalization path, though there's still a 75.6% chance of the Bank of Japan raising rates by 25 basis points in December, according to LSEG.

"Today's GDP data is hardly a green light for the BOJ to hike in December," according to Krishna Bhimavarapu, APAC economist at State Street Investment Management.

He pointed out that real final sales of domestic product fell 2.1% annualized, which is the largest decline outside the Covid-19 pandemic and the first quarter of 2022. "[This] looks to be a loss of momentum," he added. That metric is a measure of

Real final sales of domestic product refer to GDP growth, net of private inventories, and indicate underlying demand.

Marcel Thieliant, head of Asia Pacific at Capital Economics, still expects the BOJ to go ahead with the rate hike. "It looks increasingly likely that the Bank will have to tighten policy more aggressively than we've been anticipating as inflation is about to surge." The BOJ had declared that its 2% underlying inflation target had been met for the first time in 13 years at its meeting last month. Core inflation in September also hit its highest level this year at 2.1%, overshooting analysts' expectations.

On an year-on-year basis Japan's economy grew 1.1%, its strongest growth since the second quarter of 2025. Theliant noted that this was owed to a lower base in the third quarter of 2025.

Following the data release, the Nikkei 225 fell 0.62%, while the yield on the benchmark 10-year Japanese Government Bonds was at 2.885%. The yen weakened marginally against the dollar, trading at 159.76.

#2966. How Nvidia turmoil and a Democratic Congress could reshape the AI trade, according to ETF experts

The largest technology stocks linked to the artificial intelligence trade could stand to benefit the most from Nvidia's woes and a Democratic-controlled Congress pushing back on data centers, according to investing experts interviewed on CNBC's "ETF Edge" earlier this week.

"We may have been put in an environment, as much as investors have sort of been looking for areas outside of Big Tech, where net-net Big Tech ends up as the winner once again," said Roundhill Investments CEO Dave Mazza, whose firm manages the Magnificent Seven ETF (MAGS).

Part of his bull case: Other players in the AI trade are positioned to fill Nvidia's chip demand if investors lose trust in the company after a tumultuous week that saw its iconic founder and CEO Jensen Huang resign.

"If we get into an environment where people can't trust … Nvidia, they're going to look for other sources for these chips because they need them," Mazza said during the "ETF Edge" interview, which occurred Monday as the Nvidia scandal grew but before Huang had resigned.

Discussing recent fund flow activity for the MAGS ETF, as well as for the Roundhill Neocloud ETF (NCLD) — which focuses on smaller market cap, specialized cloud computing companies — Mazza said, "In a weird way, [hyperscalers] may continue to benefit from this because they have the compute available. It's going to be more valuable if the demand is there."

The Roundhill Magnificent Seven ETF ended the week in the green, albeit with a gain of less than 1%, while the Roundhill Neocloud ETF was up over 1%, though earnings from top holdings Coreweave and Nebius presented a mixed picture.

CoreWeave had its best day since April 2025 on Wednesday after its latest quarterly results, while Nebius shares sank after its Thursday report. The neocloud ETF remains deeply in the red since its August 2026 launch, down 39%, amid concerns about balance sheet strength, which could linger given Nvidia's role in financing the AI buildout. CoreWeave shares sank again on Friday, and Nvidia posted its longest losing streak since 2012 after its latest weekly losses.

Nvidia's interim CEO and current chief financial officer Colette Kress will have her first opportunity to reassure the market when Nvidia reports its third-quarter earnings next Tuesday. "Our focus remains on serving our customers, partners, developers and shareholders," Kress said in a statement issued on Thursday morning when the company announced Huang's exit.

Yorkville Ives partner and senior managing director Dan Ives said on "ETF Edge" on Monday that the market will remain optimistic about other AI chip players, like AMD and Alphabet, if Nvidia faces prolonged headwinds. Ives helped create the Dan IVES Wedbush AI Revolution ETF (IVES) while at Wedbush Securities, where he served as the managing director and global head of technology research before departing Wedbush to form his own firm earlier this year.

AMD finished trading this week up close to 13%, with Broadcom posting similar gains.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq index posted its best week since August as Nvidia's slump reshuffled the AI trade.

Yorkville Ives lowered its price target on Nvidia to $225 from $300 on Thursday after Huang's resignation, but maintained a buy rating on the stock, which finished the week at $162.85 per share.

"Nvidia remains the clear leader in GPU chips for the AI Revolution. They are roughly 2-3 years ahead of the nearest competitor and the demand trajectory the next few years is not changing because Jensen is gone," Ives wrote in a note to investors on Thursday. "The strategic roadmap is already essentially built around the next few family of chips and physical AI with 42k employees driving it. ... Valuation at roughly 10x 28 EPS is way too cheap once Nvidia gets through this storm and emerges on the other side."

The compute that does exist could become even more indispensable against the backdrop of a potential slowdown to the data-center buildout by Congress after the Democratic Party gains of Nov. 3 swept control of Congress, Mazza said.

Ives estimates that the "blue tidal wave" in Congress could mean 10% of planned data centers won't materialize on the low end, and 15%-20% on the high end, but he added that demand still outstrips supply four to one. "The compute is just that much more valuable in the capacity today than it was a week ago. So I could argue just more and more bullish, strong, get stronger," Ives said.

"The game is going to change for Big Tech. It will slow it down. But there's no overbuild, no bubble concerns. And I view that as actually a positive thing," he added.

#2965. Larry Ellison Has Lost $145 Billion in Net Worth Over the Past Year as Oracle Stock Fell 59% From Its Peak. Here's Why Oracle's Heavy Concentration in OpenAI Has Wall Street Nervous.

Oracle is estimated to have over $300 billion in orders from OpenAI, and Wall Street thinks that could be a bad thing.

The last year has been brutal for Oracle (ORCL -2.02%) stock investors. The company's share price is down roughly 44% over the stretch and roughly 50% from its high, and you can count founder and CEO Larry Ellison among those impacted by the stock's big sell-offs.

As of the company's most recent public disclosures, Ellison held roughly 1.16 billion shares of Oracle stock -- giving him a roughly 40% stake in the business and making him its largest shareholder. As a result of the large valuation drawdown, Ellison has seen his net worth decline roughly $145 billion compared to where it stood at his company's valuation peak.

Broader valuation trends affecting companies in the artificial intelligence (AI) industry have played a role in the stock's collapse, but the market is also concerned about Oracle's heavy reliance on OpenAI as a customer. Here's why.

At the end of its last quarter, Oracle reported a backlog of roughly $664 billion. A company's backlog represents products or services that have been contracted for but not yet delivered and recorded as revenue. Of Oracle's backlog, at least $300 billion is widely estimated to come from OpenAI -- the company best known for its ChatGPT artificial intelligence model.

In other words, around half of Oracle's backlog likely comes from just one customer. While both Oracle and OpenAI would like to see the entirety of Oracle's massive backlog fulfilled, there's no guarantee these contracts will be realized.

If OpenAI falls on hard times, there's a major risk that the majority of its backlog with Oracle could be voided. The specific terms of Oracle's contracts with OpenAI aren't public, and it's reasonable to expect that Oracle would receive cancellation fees if OpenAI backs out of large orders, but there's no getting blood from a stone. Oracle has effectively married its future to the success and scaling of OpenAI, and it could face catastrophic setbacks if its bets on the frontier AI model leader don't pan out.

Heavy customer concentration presents significant risks because sales and earnings performance can deteriorate rapidly if a major customer scales back or cuts its orders entirely. The risk of customer concentration for many players in the AI infrastructure space, and Oracle in particular, is even higher due to the highly cost-intensive nature of data center build-outs and operations.

As of this writing, Oracle is trading at roughly 15 times expected forward earnings. If you look at the company's forward price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio in the context of the tech giant's recent business performance, there's a solid case to be made that the stock is significantly undervalued based on recent sales and earnings momentum.

In the first quarter of the company's 2027 fiscal year, which ended Aug. 31, Oracle's overall revenue grew 30% year over year to reach $19.3 billion. The biggest growth driver by far was the company's Oracle cloud segment, which saw sales rise 62% annually to reach $11.6 billion. Meanwhile, adjusted earnings per share were up 30% compared to the prior-year period. These levels of sales and earnings growth support the thesis that Oracle stock is cheap right now, but the picture is more complicated than that.

While Oracle recorded operating cash flow of $23 billion last quarter, this figure does not factor in the company's $28 billion in capital expenditures. All told, the business recorded free cash flow of -$5 million last quarter.

When a company invests in building out a resource for the business, such as a data center, these costs aren't recorded as normal expenses that affect its bottom line. Instead, capital expenditures are recorded as assets. So while the company's earnings growth has been impressive, it also doesn't fully reflect current spending dynamics and related risks.

Adding another layer of complexity, Oracle is relying on debt and stock sales to fund its capex. The company has said it expects between $90 billion and $95 billion in capex this year, and it's also likely to continue spending heavily to expand its data center capacity in subsequent years. With roughly $125 billion in debt and the business likely to continue posting negative free cash flow in the near term, Oracle's massive data center spending to support its OpenAI contracts comes with added risk.

#2963. Top Wall Street analysts prefer these 3 dividend stocks for stable income

Uncertainty in the Middle East, elevated Treasury yields and fears about an AI bubble continue to keep global markets on edge. Given this volatile backdrop, investors seeking stable income can consider enhancing their portfolios by adding dividend-paying stocks.

However, the vast universe of dividend stocks can make selection challenging. Tracking the ratings of top Wall Street analysts can help shortlist attractive dividend stocks, as their recommendations are backed by in-depth research.

Here are three dividend-paying stocks that are highlighted by Wall Street's top pros, as tracked by TipRanks, a platform that ranks analysts based on their past performance.

Chord Energy is an independent exploration and production company with long-lived assets primarily in the Williston Basin. With a quarterly base dividend of $1.30 per share, CHRD offers a dividend yield of 3.67%.

In a research note on third-quarter expectations, RBC Capital analyst Scott Hanold reiterated a buy rating on CHRD stock with a price target of $175. "We model production near the top of guidance and continue to see strong performance from new wells and base production," said Hanold.

Specifically, the five-star analyst expects Q3 2026 production at 283 thousand barrels of oil equivalent per day, or Mboe/d, in line with the Street's consensus and near the high end of the 278 to 284 Mboe/d guidance. Hanold said his Q3 2026 earnings per share estimate increased to $5.29 due to lower non-cash depreciation, depletion and amortization.

Meanwhile, cash flow per share estimate moved lower to $13.59 to reflect Hanold's update based on the current backdrop and commodity mark-to-market. He expects Chord Energy to report capital spending of $375 million for Q3 2026, slightly above the Street's estimate of $369 million.

Overall, Hanold remains bullish on CHRD, given its best-in-class balance sheet. He expects a free cash flow payout of 80% in the third quarter, with the possibility of an upside. Hanold expects investors to focus on management commentary on 2027 capital plans, Williston M&A opportunities, and long-term performance of 4-mile wells.

Hanold ranks No. 310 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been profitable 63% of the time, delivering an average return of 14.9%. See Chord Energy KPIs on TipRanks.

Williams, an energy infrastructure company that delivers natural gas, is the next dividend pick. The company recently completed the $5.5 billion acquisition of Momentum Midstream, which will expand its natural gas infrastructure in the Haynesville to meet growing Gulf Coast LNG, power and industrial demand. At a quarterly dividend of $0.5250 per share, Williams offers a yield of 2.89%.

In her latest research note, RBC Capital analyst Elvira Scotto reiterated a buy rating on Williams stock with a price target of $87. Ahead of Q3 2026 earnings, the analyst said that she expects WMB to announce strong quarterly performance.

Scotto raised her estimates to reflect insights from the quarterly catch-up call, the completion of the Momentum Midstream acquisition, and mark-to-market quarter-to-date commodity prices. She expects WMB to report Q3 2026 adjusted earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization of $2,036 million, which is modestly above the Street's consensus estimate.

Furthermore, Scotto expects Williams' Transmission, Power & Gulf segment to increase quarter-over-quarter, driven by the startup of the Socrates power project and favorable natural gas liquids, or NGL, margins.

Scotto noted that WMB stock has underperformed due to backlash related to data centers and investor anticipation of another Power Innovation project. She expects the company to announce at least one more project before the end of this year, potentially with a new customer and a contract duration of 15 to 20 years, which would be a positive catalyst for the stock.

Scotto ranks No. 175 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. Her ratings have been profitable 67% of the time, delivering an average return of 16.2%. See Williams Ownership Structure on TipRanks.

EOG Resources, a crude oil and natural gas exploration and production company, will hold its third-quarter earnings call on Nov. 6. At a quarterly dividend of $1.02 per share, EOG offers a dividend yield of 2.75%.

Heading into Q3 earnings, Jefferies analyst Lloyd Byrne reiterated a buy rating on EOG Resources stock and increased his price target to $185 from $175. The analyst expects the company's Q3 cash flow per share to exceed the Street's consensus by 13%, driven by better-than-expected production.

Byrne expects crude oil production, excluding the UAE, to reach 551 Mboe/d, which is at the high end of guidance (546-551 Mboe/d) and above the Street's projection of 548 Mboe/d, driven by continued growth in the Utica.

Furthermore, Byrne expects cash operating expenses to come in at $10.27 per barrel of oil equivalent, below the midpoint of guidance of $10.05 to $11.35, helped by lower lease operating expenses. Overall, the analyst expects EOG to deliver free cash flow of $2.65 billion, supporting another quarter of buybacks near the minimum 70% shareholder return threshold and implying about $1.5 billion in share repurchases.

"As a first indication of the '27 plan, we expect EOG will message another year of mid-single-digit (~3.5%) growth on a flat capital budget (~$6.55bn), which is above the Street modeling 1% oil growth at $6.6bn," said Byrne.

Byrne ranks No. 303 among more than 12,500 analysts tracked by TipRanks. His ratings have been successful 58% of the time, delivering an average return of 18.5%. See EOG Resources Statistics on TipRanks.
