#2856. Cantwell urges Lutnick to stop AI chip sales to China ahead of Xi meeting

Sen. Maria Cantwell, the top Democrat on the Senate commerce committee, alleged in a letter to Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick on Sunday that the department under his leadership failed to prevent China from accessing the U.S.'s most advanced artificial intelligence chips.

In the letter, Cantwell prodded Lutnick to enforce export controls and stop the flow of AI chips to companies barred from receiving the technology. She sent the missive ahead of a planned meeting this week between U.S. officials and Chinese President Xi Jinping on the sidelines of the Asia-Pacific Economic Cooperation, in China.

Her urging comes as Washington reels from revelations that former Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang had a relationship with an alleged Chinese spy named Alice Huang. Nvidia is the leading U.S. company designing advanced AI chips.

Alice Huang was an executive at Singaporean technology company Megaspeed that allegedly smuggled restricted chips to China.

Lutnick, meanwhile, has come under fire for stalling efforts to crack down on chip sales to China and for his close personal relationship with Jensen Huang, who was a vocal opponent of export controls.

"The Department of Commerce has resisted efforts to restrict companies flagged as national security threats, weakened safeguards, and left open pathways for the PRC to train advanced AI models using sensitive American technology," Cantwell wrote to Lutnick. "These failures to carry out the law help give away America's technology advantage in AI."

The Commerce Department did not immediately respond to a request for comment on Monday.

Cantwell called on Lutnick to use his authority to enforce a 2018 law, known as the Export Control Reform Act, which gives the Commerce Department broad latitude to enforce export controls and protect sensitive American technology.

She also cites potential financial conflicts involving Lutnick's sons, who control Cantor Fitzgerald, which she said held $1.2 billion in Nvidia stock at the time of his nomination. And she notes the Trump administration's 25% fee on certain Nvidia chips sold to China, which she said could create "a perverse financial incentive to approve exports of sensitive American technology."

"America's national security and AI leadership cannot be compromised by family business interests or a policy that generates federal revenue from exporting technology critical to our competitive edge," Cantwell wrote.

Both Democratic and Republican members of Congress have ramped up pressure on Lutnick and other Commerce Department officials after a Nov. 9 New York Times report alleging the department may have slow-walked an investigation into Megaspeed.

On Thursday, Senate banking committee Chair Tim Scott, R-S.C., summoned both Huang and Lutnick to testify before his committee in December.

Lutnick has agreed to appear before the panel, Kristen Eichamer, a Commerce Department spokesperson, said in a statement last week.

And Democrats, who will take control of both the House and Senate in January, have signaled they will investigate Jensen Huang and Commerce officials.

On Wednesday, Sen. Elizabeth Warren, D-Mass., the top Democrat on the Senate banking panel, and Sen. Chris Coons, D-Del., the top Democrat on the Senate Foreign Relations subpanel with jurisdiction over East Asia and the Pacific, sent a letter to Lutnick, Secretary of State Marco Rubio and White House Chief of Staff Stephen Miller instructing them to preserve all documents and communications related to Nvidia's exports of "advanced AI chips to China or to entities that may divert such chips to China."

The letter gives all three officials until Nov. 17 to confirm that preservation directives have been issued.

#2854. Federal Reserve Board announces pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, and changes to streamline and adjust their check services

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 1:30 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Reserve Board on Thursday announced pricing, effective January 1, 2027, for payment services the Federal Reserve Banks provide to banks and credit unions, such as the clearing of checks, automated clearing house (ACH) transactions, instant payments, and wholesale payment and settlement services.

By law, the Federal Reserve must establish fees to recover the costs, including imputed costs, of providing payment services over the long run. The Federal Reserve expects to recover 101.5 percent of actual and imputed expenses in 2027, including the return on equity that would have been earned if a private-sector firm provided the services. Overall, price changes for 2027 will result in an estimated 1.4 percent average price increase for established, mature services.

In addition, the Federal Reserve Banks announced changes to streamline and adjust their check services. As described in the request for information on check services issued last year, the volume of checks processed by the Federal Reserve Banks is declining, but checks remain an important payment instrument for many consumers and businesses. The changes were informed by public feedback and ensure that the Federal Reserve Banks continue to provide check services efficiently. The adjustments include optimizing check processing windows during low-volume periods, increasing fees that banks and credit unions pay to process checks, and other operational updates. These changes do not impact consumers' and businesses' ability to pay with or receive checks and only impact how banks and credit unions process checks with Federal Reserve Banks.

The entire 2027 fee schedule for all of the Federal Reserve's payment services is published on FRBservices.org®. Additional information on the Federal Reserve's payments services, including service usage statistics, is available on the Board's website.

#2852. Is a bottom in for the Nasdaq-100? Charts to watch

(See the video above for Frank's complete chart breakdown.)

Is the Nasdaq-100's recent bounce a sign that the bottom is in? We're going to look at several charts to help answer that question.

First, let's remember how we got here. In September, the NDX broke out from a bullish cup-and-handle pattern, producing a 33,200-upside target. That breakout quickly failed, however, and gave way to a bearish pattern that broke down aggressively two weeks ago.

The decline brought the index relatively close to its 26,350 downside target before the current rebound began.

Zooming out, it's telling to see where the bounce originated. Three important forms of support converged in the 25,000-26,000 area:

This confluence of support helped encourage buying after a roughly 15% decline.

For perspective, we also see what larger drawdowns of 20%, 30%, 40%, and 50% would look like … a lot worse.

The following chart tracks NDX drawdowns from 52-week highs going back to just after the financial crisis. Only a handful of declines exceeded 20%, and three produced relatively quick reversals: late 2018, the Covid lows in 2020 and April 2025.

Historically, buying into declines of approximately 15%–25% has generally paid off, even when additional downside occurred first (yellow highlights).

The chart also highlights previous declines of 10%–16% in green, putting the current pullback into perspective.

The major exception was 2022, when the NDX eventually declined nearly 36% over approximately 10 months ... a much worse outcome, which many investors are concerned could happen again now.

That raises the key question: Are we experiencing another routine correction, or the early stages of a larger bear market?

One important clue will be how far this bounce progresses.

Any rally that fails to reclaim the previous highs could establish a significant lower high. Additional lower highs would provide stronger evidence of a developing, longer downtrend.

We saw this in March 2022, when that first sharp rebound effort failed to reach the previous high before a much more severe decline followed.

The bottom line: The current bounce is encouraging, particularly given where it originated and knowing how well buying prior 10-20% drawdowns has paid off.

From here, it's all about the follow through.  We don't need to see new highs right away, but any rally from here that's met with another wave of strong selling could reignite downside momentum.

Frank Cappelleri is the founder and president of CappThesis, an independent research firm that helps active investors through time-tested chart and statistical analysis. Prior to starting CappThesis, he spent 25 years on Wall Street as an equity sales trader, technical analyst, research sales specialist and desk strategist.

#2850. Agencies extend comment period for proposed third-party risk management guidance

An official website of the United States Government

Official websites use .govA .gov website belongs to an official government organization in the United States.

Secure .gov websites use HTTPSA lock (LockLocked padlock icon) or https:// means you've safely connected to the .gov website. Share sensitive information only on official, secure websites.

The Federal Reserve, the central bank of the United States, provides the nation with a safe, flexible, and stable monetary and financial system.

Review of Monetary Policy Strategy, Tools, and Communications

Mergers, Acquisitions, and Other Applications

Financial Stability Coordination & Actions

Financial Market Utilities & Infrastructures

For release at 4:15 p.m. EST Share

The Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation, the Federal Reserve Board, the National Credit Union Administration, and the Office of the Comptroller of the Currency today announced that they will extend, until December 16, the comment period for proposed guidance on managing risks associated with third-party relationships. Comments on the proposal were originally due November 16.

The proposed guidance reflects the agencies' supervisory experience and lessons learned from examining financial institutions' third-party risk management practices. It is intended to help banks and credit unions tailor their risk management practices to the risks associated with individual third-party relationships. The proposed guidance takes a principles-based approach and, like all supervisory guidance, is non-binding.

When finalized, the federal bank regulatory agencies plan to rescind existing third-party risk management guidance and replace it with the finalized guidance to promote consistency and prudent innovation in the banking industry. NCUA also plans to rescind its existing third-party risk management guidance and will conduct its own reviews to update related guidance as needed.

Also today, the Federal Reserve Board separately announced that it will extend, until December 16, the comment period on a proposed third-party risk management guide specifically for Federal Reserve-supervised traditional community banks.

#2845. Lakers survive Suns’ furious rally behind Luka Doncic’s 43-point night

PHOENIX — The 21-page presentation toward the beginning of their three-day break from games came with a simple message: Even though the Lakers were tied for the best record and had the league's best offense, they could be even better.

Despite being without Austin Reaves as he allows the bruise in his left heel to recover, the Lakers showed for most of their 131-123 victory over the Suns Sunday afternoon what their better could look like.

Luka Dončić #77 of the Los Angeles Lakers reacts during an NBA game against the Phoenix Suns in Phoenix, Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. Getty Images

They also showed more progress needs to be made after they nearly blew a 31-point second-half lead.

The Lakers rode the momentum of another hot start from Luka Dončić (43 points, nine assists) and precise execution to their eighth straight victory to put them at a league-best 12-2 on the season.

Luka Dončić (77) of the Los Angeles Lakers reacts during an NBA game against the Phoenix Suns on Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026, in Phoenix. Getty Images

Dončić had 20 points of his scoring total in the first quarter, which was not only his second 20-point opening quarter of the season but also made him the only player in the league with multiple 20-point first quarters.

And after the non-Dončić minutes had been a sore spot for the Lakers for most of the season, they flipped the script on Sunday.

They won the minutes Dončić was on the bench in the second quarter, helping them take a 64-54 lead going into halftime, 101-70 lead late in the third and 104-80 lead going into the fourth.

Luka Dončić #77 of the Los Angeles Lakers controls the ball against Jordan Goodwin #23 of the Phoenix Suns in Phoenix on Sunday, Nov. 15, 2026. Getty Images

Dončić's late-game offensive heroics, in addition to the Suns missing three free throws in the final two minutes, ensured the Lakers didn't relinquish the lead and held on for the wire-to-wire victory.

Quentin Grimes (22 points) came up with an offensive rebound and banked in a 3-pointer with 34 seconds left to ice the game for the Lakers.

Walker Kessler (14 points, 12 rebounds), Matisse Thybulle (12 points) and Jake LaRavia (12 points) all scored at least 12 points, putting all of the Lakers' starters in double figures in scoring.

Collin Sexton added 16 points and three assists off the bench.

#2838. NBA News: Jaylen Clark out 2 weeks

After an abysmal performance against a basement-dwelling Chicago Bulls team on Saturday night, things got worse the next day. The Minnesota Timberwolves officially announced that Jaylen Clark, our favorite try-hard defensive stopper, would miss the next two weeks at minimum with a right hamstring strain.

Here's the play where Clark got injured early in the second quarter, making a hustle play and diving into the Minnesota bench.

This is certainly something that will be a significant blow to the Wolves. Though Clark averages just over 13 minutes per game, he is fourth on the team in defensive rating (100.3). He's been one of, if not the best, on-ball defender for Minnesota. He constantly injects energy into his team when they need it most. Jaylen has also been a 36% three-point shooter so far this season, acting as a valuable release valve on the perimeter.

"Clark County" will miss at least the next eight games in the next 14 days before he's reevaluated. Soft tissue injuries are notoriously easy to reaggravate, thus it's safe to expect he will miss well over double-digit games. When you consider Jaylen's style of play – lots of quick burst lateral movement – he could be on the shelf for at least a month next to Anthony Edwards and Donte DiVincenzo.

So who will step up in the rotation from the depth chart?

I have long since been a fan of two-way guard Zyon Pullin, but he is certainly an offense-oriented player who doesn't really provide what Clark does. Rookie Isaiah Evans is earning his stripes in the G League and likely one of the less effective defensive players on the roster. I could easily see Finch divvying Jaylen's minutes over to currently involved players like Bones Hyland and Terrence Shannon Jr.

But can I make a quick case for "no longer the worst player in the NBA," Cody Williams?

The younger Williams brother has appeared in just seven of 13 games for the Wolves this season, all in "development time." However, he did show multiple flashes of his potential during the preseason. Cody impressed in a spot preseason start against the reigning champion New York Knicks, locking up 2026 Finals MVP Jalen Brunson.

Zach Lowe had a lot of positive things to say about Williams, including his defensive improvement, at the end of last season. I'm not calling for Williams to soak up Clark's entire 13 minute role, but he fits what Minnesota is losing in Clark the most. Here's a list of guards the Wolves will face in the next two to three weeks:

There will certainly be times where LaMelo Ball, Jaden McDaniels, Ayo Dosunmu, or Bones Hyland either falls into foul trouble or just need relief guarding those difficult assignments. When that happens, the need to get an active body who is athletic and long will be especially important.

This is the opportunity that many players in Cody's shoes hope for. He's still just 20 years old (soon to be 21 this week), but has years of experience under his belt for a tanking team. Minnesota will need his services out of desperation and he can shed that early career label as a draft bust. It's the perfect storm of supply meeting demand.

Williams also wears jersey number 9 which, as we all know, is a magical number.

If it were up to you, who would you most like to see replace Clark's minutes? Are you on the Cody Caboose like me? Or do you prefer another option? What is Ricky Rubio up to these days?

#2835. A trading strategy to ride out the Nvidia uncertainty and generate income in the process

Jensen Huang's abrupt resignation has sent shockwaves through the tech sector, dragging Nvidia's (NVDA) stock down roughly 30% since Oct. 6. The market despises uncertainty, and losing a visionary founder, particularly one without a well-telegraphed succession plan, makes it worse.

However, for opportunistic investors, this panic has made the stock cheaper (obviously) while options have become significantly more expensive. Trading in the $160s, Nvidia's valuation has taken a big haircut, yet its underlying business fundamentals remain largely intact. While Huang was the architect of Nvidia's AI dominance, the company's moat — superior GPUs, the CUDA software ecosystem, and networking expertise — does not walk out the door with him. The company's structural advantage in the global artificial intelligence rollout persists.

Buying good companies at a discount is investing 101, but the current market dynamics may do something else for those who use options.  Huang's departure guarantees that Nvidia will be operating under a cloud of transition for the foreseeable future. Executing a permanent CEO search while navigating global regulatory scrutiny takes time. Consequently, Nvidia's implied volatility (IV)—the market's pricing of expected future price swings — has spiked to highs not seen since the tariff tantrum in the first half of 2025, and this elevated IV is highly likely to persist for several months as the leadership drama slowly unfolds.

This prolonged period of high implied volatility can be a goldmine for option sellers, specifically those executing options overlays such as covered call strategies. When you buy NVDA shares at these depressed levels and simultaneously sell out-of-the-money call options against your position, the premiums you collect are directly tied to IV. Because fear and transition-related uncertainty have dramatically inflated these option prices, the income generated from selling covered calls is now significantly higher than usual.

This lowers the risk profile of holding the stock considerably over time. Consistently collecting these inflated premiums systematically lowers your overall cost basis in the underlying shares. If the stock trades sideways or recovers slowly during the CEO search, you are generating an outsized cash yield on a stagnant asset. If the stock experiences further downside turbulence, the heavy premium income acts as a substantial downside buffer, meaningfully reducing the strategy's overall risk over time.

By deploying a persistent covered call strategy, investors can leverage the exact fear driving the stock down to generate robust, risk-reducing income while maintaining exposure to the undeniable future of AI.

#2834. San Antonio vs. Utah, Final Score: Harper leads Spurs’ final push past Jazz, 110-102

Ascending star Dylan Harper scored 16 of his team's last 24 points to help San Antonio (12-2) claim its 12th straight victory and third straight this season over the Jazz. With the game still very much in doubt, Victor Wembanyama hit a fadeaway jumper and blocked a Keyonte George floater, and Keldon Johnson saved the ensuing carom from falling out of bounds and chased down an offensive rebound at the other end and scored a putback to put the Spurs up four in the penultimate seconds.

In a game with an exasperating amount of runs that were often one after another, Utah staged a 19-7 run spanning hte third and fourth quarters to erase the Spurs' biggest lead of the night (9) and get into the driver's seat until the visitors made that final 24-13 push.

Harper (31 points, 9 assists, and 4 assists) single-handedly willed San Antonio to victory with audacious and self-assured shotmaking throughout those critical fourth quarter minutes. Wembanyama (23 points, 9 rebounds, 5 assists, and 4 blocks) carried the offenisve production throughout the first three quarters. Stephon Castle (20 points, 9 assists, and 4 steals) and reserve Jordan McLaughlin (10 points) assured Fox wouldn't be missed TOO much. Johnson (11 points and 10 rebounds) was a menacing presence for the Spurs in the second half and especially in the waning moments.

Mo Bamba (26 points [6-for-6 from three], 5 rebounds, and 4 assists) performed admirably again in the absence of the star-studded Jazz starting bigs. Backup Brice Sensabaugh (14 points and 7 rebounds) and rookie Darryn Peterson (17 points, 5 rebounds, and 4 assists) led a group of contributors that supplied plentiful support for Bamba and made up for an off-night for Keyonte George (13 points, 8 assists, and 4 rebounds).

The teams mirrored each other over the opening minutes – with Wembanyama and Bamba taking center stage before Rutgers' Harper and Bailey exchanged buckets. While the bigs' turned away several shots at or near the rim, the Jazz swingmen made a flurry of threes to get themselves out front. Despite those outside bursts, the Spurs stayed neck-and-neck with their counterparts. While the hosts shot an astounding 7-for-10 fron behind the line, they still only went to the second up two, after McLaughlin kickstarted a closing 8-0 run to end the first quarter.

Bamba's third and fourth threes (without a miss) created some space between his team and San Antonio. While the teams continued to shoot well from the field, Utah's proficiency from distance made up the bulk of their advantage. After opposing 9-0 runs, the Jazz took advantage of San Antonio's errant shooting to leap back into the lead. Utah's perimeter men seemed to make quite a few shots late in the clock. Wembanyama rescued a handful of possessions with his shooting and playmaking and the Spurs went to halftime slightly up 58-55.

Coming out of the break, Castle figured in one each of the Spurs' first four scores – two field goals, two freebies, and one assist. Rookie Peterson dazzled with his ability to score from different spots on the floor. Bamba and Bradley resembled Utah legend Thurl Bailey by knocking down shot after shot. A 10-0 run by San Antonio got them to 79-70. Bamba's fifth straight three without a miss tied things right back at 79. San Antonio nearly went the rest of the quarter without scoring again and the teams were deadlocked at 81 going to the final 12 minutes.

#2833. Warren Buffett donates $5.5 billion, says kindness 'compounds' in Thanksgiving letter

Billionaire Warren Buffett donated Berkshire Hathaway shares worth about $5.5 billion to four philanthropic foundations, stepping up his annual charitable giving from the prior year.

Buffett, 96, converted 6,150 Class A Berkshire shares into 9,225,000 Class B shares and gave 3,690,000 of those shares to The Susan Thompson Buffett Foundation, and 1,845,000 shares each to The Sherwood Foundation, The Howard G. Buffett Foundation and NoVo Foundation — the foundations run by his three children, Susie, Howard and Peter Buffett.

Following the donations, the chairman emeritus of Berkshire still owns 182,140 Class A shares — roughly $162 billion.

"Last Thanksgiving I wrote that kindness is costless but also priceless. A year later, I would only add that it compounds, and at a better rate than anything Charlie and I ever bought," wrote Buffett, who was succeeded as chairman in September by his son Howard, in his annual Thanksgiving message.

The letter was much shorter than his message last Thanksgiving. Charlie Munger, former vice chairman of Berkshire, passed away in November 2023.

Berkshire shares have been on a tear recently, up more than 19% since the start of the fourth quarter and ending last week with four consecutive record high closes. That compares with the S&P 500's decline of 1.2% so far for the fourth quarter. Berkshire has been seen as a safe haven as investors have worried about the artificial intelligence buildout, corporate drama and geopolitical risk.

The shares are now outperforming the S&P 500 for the year by about 7.5 percentage points.

In his letter, Buffett reiterated that all of his Berkshire shares eventually will be donated to the four foundations, with the goal being to increase the size of the grants each year.

Buffett wrote that the gifts were a signal of his confidence not only in his three children, but also in Berkshire CEO Greg Abel:

"My children have given thoughtfully to worthy causes for decades, and I trust 100% both their abilities and their fidelity. ... You heard plenty from me in September about my confidence in Berkshire. That confidence is as strong as ever," he said.

Referring to Munger and himself, Buffett added, "Both of us received beyond measurement costless benefits from a few teachers such as Ben Graham, Joe Rosenfield and Tom Murphy. Because of this we not only prospered mightily but were able to pass along many of the benefits we received."

"Warren continues to share his wisdom with us all during this holiday season. His example reflects the kindness he encourages in all of us," said CEO Abel of the letter.

Buffett closes his message with this advice: "This Thanksgiving, thank one of those selfless people in your own life. Better yet, be a benefactor for someone else."

#2832. What the reported Drake Maye injury means for the Patriots

The New England Patriots did not just lose their Week 10 game to the Detroit Lions, they also saw their starting quarterback depart early in the 35-10 contest. And not just that: Drake Maye is believed to have suffered a "significant lower leg injury" that is reportedly expected to keep him out an extended period of time and might even end his season.

While further tests will be conducted to determine a return timeline, Maye's immediate future is, at the very least, in question. What does this mean for the Patriots? Let's find out.

The Patriots are 5-5 and despite dropping out of the playoff picture on Sunday remain in the race for a postseason spot in the AFC. However, if Maye is indeed forced to miss extended time or even the rest of the season, the outlook changes drastically: given all of the issues, this does not look like a playoff-caliber team without its Pro Bowl-caliber quarterback.

At least publicly, the Patriots and head coach Mike Vrabel won't care. We will hear the classic "next man up" and "everybody is preparing like a starter" lines, while there will be no outward readjustment of the goals.

Make no mistake, however. This is the worst-case scenario for the Patriots and their 2026 season. It might not mean they will go 0-7 down the stretch, especially if Maye does come back, but the sights might have to be set on 2027 sooner rather than later.

Sunday's game against the Lions might not have been an accurate reflection of their capabilities, but that doesn't mean the Patriots' offensive outlook is not bleak. Fact is, after all, that Tommy DeVito is a serious downgrade from Drake Maye.

Download the SB Nation app on iOS or Android.

That said, the Patriots are still expected to rely on their backup for however long Maye will ultimately be out. Rookie Behren Morton, who served as the emergency third option throughout the season thus far, will be elevated to the No. 2 spot with a third passer potentially being added to the roster or practice squad if need be.

DeVito, 28, entered the NFL as an undrafted rookie with the New York Giants in 2023. He started eight total games over two year but was waived in 2025. He subsequently joined the Patriots in a backup role, serving as the third passer behind Maye and Joshua Dobbs. When Dobbs was let go earlier in the year, DeVito officially took over the backup spot.

As such, he saw minimal action over the first nine weeks of the season. Against the Lions, however, he played most of the game and finished with 12 completions on 24 attempts for 81 yards with a touchdown and an interception. The Patriots scored once with him at the helm and punted eight times, including seven three-and-outs.

However long the Patriots' medical staff determines Maye will be out might also have an impact on other injury situations. Frankly, if the starter is sidelined and the losses keep piling up, there would be no need for any players to be rushed back from injury. There shouldn't be any way, to be honest, but the threshold of "pushing through" an injury might be reconfigured should Maye indeed be out an extended period of time.

That "pushing through" process, however, appears to be an integral part of New England's culture this season. Multiple players are currently banged up or still dealing with the effects of injuries they suffered earlier; the bye week will come handy in that regard.

If the overall goals begin to shift, however, so might the culture — for better or worse. It will be on Mike Vrabel, who was hired in 2025 in large part due to his abilities as a leader and team-builder, to make sure the locker room sees through this latest challenge in a season full of them.

As of Monday morning, three of the Patriots' final seven games of the season are scheduled as prime time games. One of those is a realistic candidate to be flexed out of its spot, and would have been so even before Maye got hurt: the Sunday night game against the Los Angeles Chargers in Week 12.

That being said, with Thanksgiving that weekend and games taking place on Thursday and Friday as well, the alternatives are actually minimal. Unless the league wants to flip it to the 1 p.m. ET spot currently occupied by Raiders-Browns, only the 4:25 p.m. ET contest between the Seahawks and 49ers makes sense. Whether or not FOX will give that one up, however, will be seen.

As for the other prime time games on the schedule, they likely will stay in their slots due to their nature as Thursday (Week 14 vs. Vikings) and Monday (Week 15 vs. Chiefs) games. Still, the two undetermined slots in Weeks 17 (vs. Broncos) and 18 (vs. Dolphins) could now very well end up as 1 p.m. games.

It could very well be tied to the "pushing through" culture at One Patriot Place, but on multiple occasions this season players have come back sooner than their initially-reported injuries made it sound. Running back Rhamondre Stevenson is the latest example: he was expected to miss multiple weeks due to the knee injury he suffered in Week 9 against Green Bay, but was back at practice four days later and ended up playing 26 snaps against the Lions.

With that in mind, Monday's reports might have to be taken with a grain of salt. With the bye week coming up, the Patriots do have some extra time at their hands for recovery.

At the end of the day, of course, they would be wise to play the long game with Maye. Yes, the playoffs are still a distinct possibility even at 5-5, but they need to be careful with their franchise quarterback.

#2831. Luka Dončić scores 43, leading Lakers over Suns for eighth consecutive win, tying for NBA's top record

Luka Dončić continued his scorching start to the 2026-27 NBA season, topping the 40-point mark for the seventh time in 12 games in the Los Angeles Lakers' 131-123 win over the Phoenix Suns on Sunday. He currently leads the NBA in scoring at 38.6 points per game.

Dončić scored 20 of his 43 points in the first quarter, hitting three 3-pointers as he shot 5-for-10 and the Lakers took a 33-24 lead. That advantage increased to 54-37 with five minutes to go before halftime on a Jake LaRavia 3. Yet the Suns closed out the second quarter on a 12-3 run to trim the Lakers' lead to 61-54.

In the third quarter, the Lakers started hot from behind the arc, hitting three 3-pointers (two by Quentin Grimes) as part of a 13-3 run and boosted their lead to 20 points. Eventually, that margin became 31 points with under two minutes remaining in the quarter. Dončić added 12 points to put him at 37 points going into the final 12 minutes.

Phoenix recovered to give the Lakers a game in the fourth quarter, hitting four 3-point attempts to begin the frame. A Walker Kessler tip-in dunk restored the Lakers' advantage to 113-92 with just under nine minutes remaining.

However, the Suns responded with a 15-1 surge, fueled by six points from rookie Koa Peat. A 3 from Ryan Dunn cut the Lakers' lead to 117-111 at the 4:40 mark. Collin Gillespie drove to the basket for a layup that pulled Phoenix to within five points with 1:05 left in regulation.

But another 3 from Grimes effectively put the game away for the Lakers and Dončić closed out the scoring with two free throws. However, he told sideline reporter Roqui Theus afterward that the plan was for him to sit out the fourth quarter until the Suns made the game close.

"I wasn't planning on playing the fourth quarter," Dončić said. "We gotta get better at that. … Once we get up, we gotta stay there."

The Lakers played without Austin Reaves, who missed the game due to a bone bruise in his left heel.

Overall, Dončić shot 13-of-28 from the floor, while making 12-of-14 free throws. He also notched nine assists in his 43 minutes of play. Grimes followed with 22 points, shooting 5-for-6 on 3-pointers, with 6 assists and 2 steals. Collin Sexton contributed 16 points off the bench and Kessler added 14 points with 12 rebounds.

For the Suns, Gillespie finished with 26 points (shooting 5-for-10 on 3s), 4 assists and 4 rebounds. Luke Kennard added 18 as the Suns got 78 points from their reserves. Devin Booker led the Phoenix starters with 17 points and 7 assists.

The Lakers have won eight consecutive games and improved their record to 12-2, tying the San Antonio Spurs for the NBA's best record. (The Spurs defeated the Utah Jazz on Sunday, 110-102.) Their next game is Tuesday versus the Charlotte Hornets (4-9).

#2830. Nvidia fallout: Commerce cracks down on third-country loophole that allowed China to use computing power

The Commerce Department moved on Monday to close a loophole that has let Chinese companies lease Nvidia's advanced computing power from data centers in third countries, effectively circumventing controls on sensitive chip technology being exported to China.

Commerce's action, an interim final rule issued by its Bureau of Industry and Security, is the first U.S. policy response to the scandal at Nvidia that led CEO Jensen Huang to resign last week.

The department, in a press release, said that the BIS rule was "written by and at the direction of" the Treasury Department and the National Security Council.

The rule came after rising criticism of Commerce Secretary Howard Lutnick, who has been accused of not doing enough to prevent possible diversion of Nvidia's most advanced chips to China in violation of American export controls.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent, in an interview on Friday with CNBC's "Squawk Box," blamed the Commerce Department for not putting Megaspeed, the Singapore-based company at the center the Nvidia scandal, on the so-called entity list, which would restrict certain exports to it.

Secretary of State Marco Rubio, who, as national security advisor, leads the National Security Council, warned President Donald Trump last spring that Huang was in a close relationship with Megaspeed executive Alice Huang, whom U.S. intelligence believed to be a Chinese spy, The New York Times reported on Nov. 8. Jensen Huang, who is not related to Alice, resigned last week after other reports said he had affairs with her and a subordinate at Nvidia.

The Commerce Department, in the notifice of the IFR on Monday, said that it "is establishing new rules to prevent certain foreign actors from leveraging advanced computing power based on U.S. technology to threaten U.S. national security and foreign policy interests."

These restrictions will undermine the ability of companies headquartered in countries of concern from using U.S. advanced Super Intelligence (SI) semiconductors to train advanced SI models," the rule said.

The rule also says that a license will now "be required to export, reexport, or transfer (in-country) advanced semiconductors to Indonesia, Malaysia, Singapore, Thailand, or Brazil (and any additional country designated by the Secretary of Commerce, in consultation with the Secretary of State or the Assistant to the President for National Security Affairs) – countries which present a higher-than-normal risk of diversion."

The rule was published a day after Sen. Maria Cantwell of Washington state, the ranking Democrat on the Committee on Commerce, Science and Transportation, in a letter to Lutnick said that Chinese companies such as Alibaba and ByteDance have "used overseas data centers equipped with advanced Nvidia chips to train advanced AI models."

"These companies are exploiting a loophole in U.S. export controls by using advanced American chips in foreign data centers even when those chips are restricted from the [People's Republic of China]," Cantwell wrote.

"Despite warnings from the Bureau of Industry and Security that certain arrangements may violate existing restrictions, the Department of Commerce failed to close this loophole, allowing PRC companies to advance their AI capabilities using sensitive American technology."

Chiefs wide receiver Josh Downs didn't waste any time making an impact for his new team.

#2828. Game Recap: Furious comeback by Suns bench falls short in 131-123 loss

The Lakers came out of the gates hot, and the Suns did not. From there, every time the Suns started to put together a run, the Lakers answered with a bigger run. And while the bench gave the Suns some hope with a furious comeback attempt in the fourth quarter, the Suns dug themselves far too big a hole to climb out of, ultimately falling to the Lakers at home, 131-123.

If the Suns had any hope of an upset, they needed to slow down the Lakers' potent offense, and they failed to do that for much of the afternoon. The Lakers came out of the gates hot and did not really slow down. Luka scored 20 in the first quarter on the way to a 43-point afternoon (together with nine assists), but the Suns' inability to stop Lakers not named Luka, inability to keep the Lakers off the offensive boards, and inability to stop the three is what ultimately did the team in.

Quentin Grimes scored 22 points for the Lakers, including 11 in the third quarter, while shooting 5-of-6 from three. The Lakers grabbed 12 offensive rebounds – with Walker Kessler having six of them – which led to 21 second-chance points. And the Lakers shot 16-of-41 (39%) from three, with Luka and Grimes combining to hit 10 of them.

For the Suns, the story was the fact that the starters dug such a deep hole that the bench could not get them out. Devin Booker was 4-14, Jalen Green was 4-11, and Dillon Brooks was 0-of-2 (what?!). The Suns actually out-rebounded the Lakers (42 to 40) and grabbed more offensive rebounds than the Lakers (14 to 12), but not thanks to the starters. The bench grabbed 29 of the Suns' 42 rebounds and 12 of their 14 offensive rebounds. But the Suns' bench not only outperformed the starters on the boards, but they also outperformed them overall.

Collin Gillespie led the Suns in scoring with 26 points (including 5-of-10 from three). Jordan Goodwin led the team in rebounds, with seven. Ryan Dunn grabbed four offensive rebounds, and Luke Kennard chipped in another three.

And let's not forget Koa Peat. Mr. Arizona sparked a furious fourth-quarter rally that saw the Suns' bench cut a 24-point Lakers lead to just five, before running out of steam down the stretch.

Overall, this game ended up being much closer than it felt throughout the afternoon. The Suns started the fourth quarter down by 24, and the bench was entirely responsible for the team nearly pulling this one out. Notably, Jordan Ott stuck with the bench throughout that quarter. He did not go back to the starters. It felt like the right move, but also truly confirms the lack of cohesiveness with the starting five.

At the end of the day, the Suns miss out on a chance to get to .500, falling to 5-7, as the Lakers win their eighth in a row to move to 12-2.

The Suns got off to the slowest start we've seen since opening night, letting the Lakers score the first 10 points of the game — eight of them by Luka — before Miles Bridges scored the team's first points with 9:24 left in the first quarter. The Lakers followed that 10-2 start by going on a 13-3 run, which left the Suns trailing by 18 after just over 5 minutes of playing time.

Jordan Ott went to the bench, and the bench and Devin Booker managed to trim that lead to 9 by the end of the quarter. A Booker jumper, a Collin Gillespie three, then a Kahman Maluach layup on a nice feed from Book sparked a 7-0 run, to make it a 23-12 game. From there, Book and Maluach were responsible for the rest of the Suns' points that quarter – with Book scoring eight and Man Man chipping in four.

Maluach flashed some of his defensive potential on a play where he challenged and altered a layup attempt by Sandro Mamukelashvili and showed a quick second jump by immediately blocking a Jarred Vanderbilt layup attempt.

At the end of the first, the Suns found themselves down 33-24.

With Luka out to start the second, the Suns needed to take advantage of the non-Luka minutes – and, at first, it looked like they did. The Suns started the quarter with a Gillespie three to cut the lead to six, but that was the closest they would get (until the fourth). The Suns ended up losing the non-Luka minutes – letting Collin Sexton score eight points in the second. By the time Luka came back in the game with 4:56 left in the half, the Suns found themselves down 17.

That lead ballooned to 19, before the Suns started chipping away again. At the 3:36 mark, they were down 19. At that time, the story of the game was the difference from three (with the Lakers being +12) and from the free-throw line (+7). The Lakers also grabbed seven offensive rebounds in the second frame alone.

The only player showing any true effort on the boards for the Suns at this juncture was Jordan Goodwin — who was fouled on two separate occasions while trying to grab offensive rebounds off missed free throws.

Despite only having 10 points from Booker in the first half, the Suns were only down 10 at halftime.

The third quarter started a lot like the first. The Lakers were scoring and we could not. After the Lakers scored the first eight points, the Suns finally got some points from Devin Booker with 9:28 left. But it continued to be all Lakers from there.

Just like Collin Sexton stepped up for the Lakers in the second quarter, the third quarter belonged to Quentin Grimes. Grimes, who came in shooting 28% from three on the season, scored 11 in the quarter, including three 3-pointers. Luka also scored in double figures in the third quarter, chipping in 12.

In the third, the Suns shot 9-of-21 overall and 2-of-11 from three, while the Lakers shot 14-of-23 overall and 5-of-9 from three.

The fourth quarter started with little hope and a 24-point deficit. The Suns came out and promptly scored the first six points, and after the Lakers got the lead back to 22, Collin Gillespie hit back-to-back threes to cut the lead to 16 with 10 minutes left.

But then the offensive rebounds started coming back. Thybulle had a tip-in off a missed three, then on the next possession, Kessler had a put-back dunk on a missed layup by Thybulle. The Suns were down 19 with 8:15 left, and Koa Peat checked in – making it look like Jordan Ott had decided to pack it in.

But Peat checking in resulted in a furious comeback attempt that made a mundane afternoon into an exciting one.

Peat immediately impacted the game, fighting off Kessler on a Suns miss, which allowed the team to keep possession, then scoring on a strong shot over Kessler.

Collin Gillespie was also not ready for the game to be called, as he drew an and-1 on the next possession, to get the Suns within 14. This brought Luka and his 37 points back into the game. The first possession of Luka's return resulted in a turnover, and Gillespie missed a step-through jumper, but Peat was there for the tip-in. Suns down 12 with just over six minutes to go.

The Suns used a 10-1 run to find themselves down single digits with 5:02 to go. And they kept chipping away.

A Ryan Dunn three cut the lead to six with 4:37 left. Jake Laravia answers with a three of his own, but then Luke Kennard drops one to make it a six-point game again, but a missed three by Laravia followed by an offensive rebound and then a BANKED in three by Lavaria sealed the game for the Lakers.

An exciting comeback, but, ultimately, a 131-123 loss — and a loss that just further magnifies the obvious disconnect amongst the Suns starters. But still, Koa Peat…

The Suns hit the road immediately for an away matchup tomorrow against the Denver Nuggets.

#2827. Berkshire breaks out to record high with fourth-quarter rally reaching 19%

(This is the Warren Buffett Watch newsletter, news and analysis on all things Warren Buffett and Berkshire Hathaway. You can sign up here to receive it every Friday evening in your inbox.)

Berkshire Hathaway's red-hot shares are ending a strong week with four consecutive fresh record high closes.

Since the rally began with the start of the fourth quarter on October 1, the Class A shares have closed at record highs 11 times as they soared 19.1%.

The much more widely held B shares closed at new highs just 10 times but are also up 19.1% from their near-term low.

The gains are in sharp contrast to the S&P 500's 1.2% dip as investors appear to be seeing Berkshire as a relatively safe haven amid worries about AI stocks and spending.

Both Berkshire classes were up 3.9% on the week as the S&P advanced by 2.5%, its best week since early August.

Berkshire's B shares are now 7.5 percentage points ahead of the benchmark for the year so far.

After erasing their post-Buffett CEO announcement losses three weeks ago, Berkshire shares are now up almost 10% since last year's shareholders meeting.

They've gained around 29% since last August's lows and are up around 18% year-to-date.

Berkshire Hathaway is due to disclose its quarterly equity portfolio snapshot on Monday when it files its Q3 13F with the SEC.

We won't know for sure until then, but numbers in the company's third quarter 10-Q, released last Saturday, indicate Berkshire continued to reduce its Bank of America stake.

It would be the ninth consecutive quarter of selling.

As of June 30, Berkshire reported holding 483.4 million BAC shares valued now at $24.7 billion, it's fifth largest equity position.

On June 30, 2024, before the selling began, Berkshire owned just over 1 billion shares valued at $41.1 billion.

Since then, the number of shares in the portfolio has decreased by 53.2%, not including any Q3 sales, while the stock price has increased by 28.3%.

If there had been no reductions, the position would be worth $52.7 billion.

The belief Berkshire sold more BAC shares in the third quarter is based on a $372 million reduction in the cost basis for equities categorized by the company as "banks, insurance, and finance."

The 10-Q also reports $2.2 billion of pretax gains from equity sales.

Barron's notes Bank of America and American Express are the only financial stocks in the portfolio that have a cost basis low enough to generate a gain like that. Amex is one of the four stocks new CEO Greg Abel indicated would be held for "decades" to come in his February letter to shareholders.

In any case, Berkshire's almost 23% stake in Amex would have required it to quickly disclose any sales.

Based on the numbers in the 10-Q, the warrants Berkshire received as part of a 2011 $5 billion investment in the bank that it used in 2017 to buy 700 million shares at $7.14 each, and BAC's average stock price in the quarter, Barron's estimates 30 to 40 million shares may have been sold.

That would be roughly in line with the 30.2 million shares sold in the second quarter.

The case isn't as good, but Barron's thinks Berkshire may have bought more Alphabet shares in the period. That's based on a $2.3 billion increase in the cost basis for the category that includes Google's parent.

Overall, it looks like it will be a relatively quiet quarter with $2.4 billion in equity purchases and $2.8 billion in sales.

In the first quarter, Berkshire bought $15.9 billion and sold $24.1 billion of equities. In the second quarter, it purchased $23.5 billion and sold $3.7 billion.

All those numbers exclude the company's buybacks of its own stock.

In a term sheet filed early Friday, Berkshire Hathaway says it is selling more than 182 billion yen ($1.1 billion) worth of bonds with maturities between 2029 and 2056 and annual interest rates between 2.6% and 4.6%.

Bloomberg points out the rates, compared to benchmarks, are higher than its previous sale this year.

It quotes a fund manager as saying market sentiment "has become more sensitive," forcing issuers to make their offerings more attractive.

In April, Berkshire sold 272 billion yen ($1.7 billion) of bonds.

Berkshire uses the proceeds from the yen-denominated bonds to protect its ongoing investments in Japanese stocks from currency fluctuations.

Warren Buffett recommends Benjamin Graham's book, "The Intelligent Investor," and outlines what he considers Graham's three most important ideas.

WARREN BUFFETT: On investment books, if you're asking about that, I would recommend the first two books that Phil Fisher wrote back around 1960, "Common Sense [Stocks] and Uncommon Profits" and the second one ["Paths to Wealth Through Common Stocks"]. They're very good books.

You know, I obviously recommend, first and foremost, [Benjamin Graham's] "The Intelligent Investor," with chapters eight and 20 are the ones that you really should read.

Two of the — well, all of the important ideas in investing, really, are in that book, because there's only about three ideas. And those — two of them are emphasized in those two chapters...

One is that — to think of yourself — to think of investing as owning a business and not buying something that wiggles around in price.

And the second one is your attitude, which ties in with that, the attitude toward the market, that's covered in chapter eight. And if you have the proper attitude toward market movements, it's an enormous help in securities.

And the final chapter is on the margin of safety, which means, don't try and drive a 9,800-pound truck over a bridge that says it's, you know, "Capacity: 10,000 pounds." But go down the road a little bit and find one that says, "Capacity: 15,000 pounds."

Berkshire market capitalization: $1,259,897,054,755

Berkshire Cash as of Sept 30: $377.4 billion (Up 3.3% from June 30)

Excluding Rail Cash and Subtracting T-Bills Payable: $352.3 billion (Down 1.9% from June 30)

Berkshire repurchased $9.6 billion of its shares in Q3 2026.

Berkshire's top holdings of disclosed publicly traded stocks in the U.S. and Japan, by market value, based on the latest closing prices.

Holdings are as of June 30, 2026, as reported in Berkshire Hathaway's 13F filing on August 14, 2026, except for:

#2824. 3 bleak thoughts after the Mavericks’ no-show in 131-94 loss at the Boston Celtics

The last three games make it clear. This season is all about managing expectations for fans of the Dallas Mavericks (7-7). But good grief.

The Mavericks dropped the second game of a back-to-back set in horrifying fashion, 131-94, in a Sunday matinee against the Boston Celtics (11-2) at TD Garden.

The unbridled optimism that came along with a surprise 7-4 start has been tempered, to say the least, after a pair of stinkers at the Minnesota Timberwolves and the Cleveland Cavaliers, followed by Sunday's scheduled loss, when the Mavs were again without star 34-year-old guard Kyrie Irving, due to injury management (rest).

The Celtics poured in three of their first four 3-point attempts to jump out to an 11-2 lead three minutes into the game, and the tone was set quickly. The Mavs didn't make their first shot from the field until Zaccharie Risacher hit a long jumper from the corner with 5:22 left in the first. The starting lineup of Cooper Flagg, Caleb Martin, Naji Marshall, Daniel Gafford and PJ Washington provided zero floor spacing on the offensive end.

And so, naturally, the Celtics' early lead swelled to 19-4 after Jordan Walsh went to the basket for six straight points on three straight possessions less than five minutes into the game. If Cooper Flagg isn't going out of his mind on offense, the roster as constructed for Sunday's game just doesn't stand a chance against the league's best teams.

Boston canned seven triples in the first quarter, while the Mavs shot an abysmal 2-of-18 from the field. The game was basically over as the Celtics led by an appalling 42-9 count after one. It goes without saying that the nine points were the lowest scoring output for the Mavs of the young 2026-27 season, but what was worse is that the team appeared to let it affect their effort on defense, as well.

The Mavericks woke up a little in the second quarter, but waking up after you're down 35 points is not worth a whole hell of a lot. A little 9-0 run late in the frame pulled the Mavs to within 59-34, but it seems laughable to even mention developments like that in the course of such a monumental shellacking.

Dallas turned the ball over 12 times in the first half to offset the team's five second-quarter 3-point makes, and Boston carried a lopsided 67-37 lead into the halftime break.

Flagg's early run of brilliance to start the season has hit a hard car crash of a stop. He shot a combined 11-of-31 in the two losses leading up to Sunday's game at Boston, and he followed that up by missing all three of his first-quarter shot attempts.

We see apex Flagg, as we've learned, when he plays next to competent guards, and that was simply not the case against the Celtics. The starting lineup at Boston featured exactly zero guards, and when Walter Clayton Jr. and Marcus Sasser entered halfway into the first, they offered little to no help on either end. Clayton came back in the second and scored eight points on two made 3-pointers, but the damage had been done at that point.

The Celtics' defense concentrated all their efforts into containing Flagg, because Flagg was the only viable source of offense left on Dallas' injury-riddled roster. He finally connected on his first field goal early in the second, on the low block through the foul of Payton Pritchard. But after two more 3-balls from the Celtics on the other end, the Celtics' lead had ballooned to 50-15 with nine minutes left in the first half.

There will be angst, and a certain amount of angst is warranted here, but circumstances aren't helping the 19-year-old star, either. He scored just five points in the first half and finished with 17 on 5-of-15 shooting in the loss.

Let's just get this out of the way and move on, shall we? Jayson Tatum torched the Mavericks for 21 of his game-high 32 points in the third quarter to lead way for the Celtics in the win. Derrick White added 16. Eight different Celtics made two or more 3-pointers, including Tatum, who put in five of them.

Risacher led the Mavericks with 18 off the bench on 8-of-9 shooting.

#2823. Ravens discussion: loss to Chargers would be most embarrassing since…?

The Ravens have some bad losses on their ledger over the last several years.

But even with all the blown leads and second-half collapses in recent years, falling to the Chargers – the winless Chargers – on Monday night might be a new low. The following is true of Jim Harbaugh's squad this year:

-98 point differential through eight games…

…good for a 12.25-point average margin of defeat

18.9% of offensive drives end in a turnover

The Chargers will also be without star wide receiver Ladd McConkey on Monday night, while a few other key players are questionable.

All of these factors should tip the scales in the Ravens' direction, even with Jacoby Brissett set to make his debut. But there is a reason winless seasons are rare. This is an Any Given Sunday league, wherein last week's defeat is forgotten in the fog of a new war. The Chargers are probably going to beat someone eventually. Wouldn't it be so spot-on for the Ravens' 2026 season if it happened in Baltimore on Monday night?

That would almost certanily be the Ravens' most embarrassing loss this season. That's saying something after off three straight defeats of 22 points or more, but those came against three of the best teams in the AFC with a combination of Tyler Huntley and Joe Fagnano under center. Last year's 44-10 loss to the Texans looms large given the margin, but what more were you expecting from Cooper Rush, a bad O-line against a fiercesome Houston front, and a suddenly banged-up defense?

My pick: Week 10 of the 2021 season, a 22-10 loss to the Dolphins. The Ravens had no answers for Brian Flores' defense. Lamar Jackson could not summon his usual magic to dance away from pressure and make plays downfield, and Baltimore's offense walked on the field like lambs to the slaughter every drive. A solid showing from the defense – ironically, against Brissett for much of the game – was not enough to rescue one of the worst showings of Jackson's career.

Dishonorable mention: Come to think of it, Week 2 of the 2022 season – Mike Macdonald's second game as defensive coordinator – was an embarrassing loss, too. The Ravens seem to only throttle the Dolphins or melt down in spectacular fashion.

#2820. Celtics throttle Mavericks, 131-94, thanks to magnificent 42-9 1st quarter

Some NBA games are won late in the fourth quarter, with dramatic twists and turns in the final seconds and elite shotmaking from the game's brightest stars.

Sunday was not one of those games. This one was over before many fans settled into their seats, as the Celtics led by 33 through 1 en route to a dominant 131-94 victory at TD Garden.

"We were playing well, forcing them into shots we want them to take, and they missed some shots," said Celtics coach Joe Mazzulla.

Boston (11-2) has now won six straight, including four by 20 or more, and is still in first place in the Eastern Conference. Dallas (7-7), playing without Kyrie Irving (knee) on the second night of a back to back, looked outmatched in every facet early.

Jayson Tatum led the way with 32 points (11-of-18 shooting), 6 rebounds and 6 assists. Boston shot 51 percent as a team, leaving no doubt and improving to 6-0 at home on the season.

The Celtics absolutely dominated the first quarter, building a 42-9 advantage through 1 with a truly spectacular performance. Yes, it was the largest lead after the first quarter in franchise history. It felt like it, too.

Boston shot 59 percent from the floor and 54 percent from 3, while Dallas was at 11 percent from the field and 0 percent from distance. The Celtics racked up 14 points in the paint, compared to 2 for the Mavericks, and won the rebounding battle, 16-10.

Boston could do no wrong. Dallas could do no right.

"We tried to shrink the floor as much as possible and cause havoc," said Celtics wing Jordan Walsh.

Walsh played with conviction and purpose and helped set the tone early, and Derrick White (16 points) found a rhythm early to help extend the lead. Seemingly every shot the Celtics threw up went in, as the action resembled a varsity team scrimmaging a junior varsity team.

The Mavericks found their footing in the second quarter and chipped away, but the Celtics preserved a 67-37 lead heading into halftime following a relatively even second quarter.

"Obviously it's easy to really bring it when it's a close game, or things like that," Tatum said. "Just trying to challenge our mindset of we're up 25 to 30, can you try to put the game away? I think we did a really good job of that today."

Tatum, Pritchard and White continued to flourish, and Sam Hauser provided strong minutes off the bench. Zaccharie Risacher (18 points) paced Dallas with 9 in the half, but Cooper Flagg (17) struggled and the Mavericks turned it over six more times in the quarter.

Eleven players saw the floor for Boston and nine scored, led by White with 11.

The Celtics kept their target practice going in the third quarter, as Paul George and Walsh hit back-to-back corner 3's to push the margin to 34. Neemias Queta added one of his own from the other corner to improve to 4 of 5 from distance on the season.

"His growth and his role has gone kind of under the radar," Mazzulla said of Queta. "He's more important than just with defense. He's important in the locker room, he's important on the offensive end. He continues to get better and better. It's easy to overlook it because of how easy he makes it."

Flagg got going in the third, but Tatum continued to cook and kept the lead hovering around 30 most of the way.

Tatum drilled a step-back 3 with 4.3 seconds left to make it 104-71 through three. He finished the quarter with 21 and continued to look like pre-injury Tatum.

The Celtics started the fourth with Baylor Scheierman, Devin Carter, Ron Harper Jr., Hugo González and Luka Garza, and they continued to play the brand of basketball that got Boston to that point.

Harper Jr. converted inside, Scheierman and Harper Jr. swished consecutive 3's and Garza delivered inside to push it to 40, 117-77, with 8:58 to go.

"The start of the fourth quarter, we made them call the first timeout," Mazzulla said.

Gino Time came with 2:51 remaining and the Celtics up 36. The win streak is alive, and the Celtics are looking very much like contenders.

#2818. NFL playoff picture after the Browns join the party in Week 10

We have a new team in the playoff party after Sunday's action.

Week 10 of the 2026 NFL season is not over yet, but after a thrilling win over the Houston Texans, the Cleveland Browns are now on the No. 7 line in the AFC. If the playoffs began today, Cleveland would be on the road to take on the Kansas City Chiefs on Wild Card Weekend.

While there is a long way to go until matchups are officially set, this is another example of why the Browns are one of the biggest surprises this season.

Here is the full playoff picture as Week 10 draws to a close.

Here is the AFC playoff picture following all the Sunday games, including the big matchup between the Cincinnati Bengals and the Pittsburgh Steelers on Sunday night:

1. Buffalo Bills (8-1)2. Kansas City Chiefs (7-2)3. Jacksonville Jaguars (7-2)4. Cincinnati Bengals (7-2)5. Denver Broncos (7-2)6. Pittsburgh Steelers (6-3)7. Cleveland Browns (6-4)

Jacksonville takes the No. 3 spot over Cincinnati due to a head-to-head win. Cincinnati slots in at No. 4, and the Broncos take the No. 5 spot.

The other big change? The New England Patriots fall out of the No. 7 spot after Sunday's loss to the Detroit Lions, and the Cleveland Browns are now in following a dramatic win over the Houston Texans.

#2814. The Optimist: What the Turducken was that, refs?

Despite the closeness of yesterday's 20-17 victory over the New Orleans Saints, the Panthers established themselves as the team to beat in the NFC South. A dubious honor, to be sure, but it's where we are now. The Tampa Bay Buccaneers are the only thing that resembles a threat in this division and I'm not confident that what we saw against them in Week 7 will happen again in Week 12. I guess we'll see.

The Panthers overcame aggressively one-sided officiated, middling quarterback play from Bryce Young, baffling editorial and marketing decisions by Fox Sports, and inconsistent kicking from Ryan Fitzgerald to do something they couldn't manage last season: they won a game to take control of the division. They also snapped a three game losing streak, extended their streak of not sending anybody to injured reserve to two weeks, and are set to keep bringing key players back from injuries over the next several weeks.

That said, it's not all sunshine and beignets for the Panthers after conquering the Saints in New Orleans. We can't pretend like that wasn't a rough game even outside of the liberal interpretations of several rules that the officials were using. There are four things that need to be addressed coming out of Week 10 that need to be addressed more than anything else if the Panthers want to win any more games this season.

Play-calling and execution in the red zone and in short yardage situations have been a challenge all season. The Saints opening drive ended with an interception that, after one offensive play, became first and ten at the New Orleans 11 yard line. The Panthers walked away with zero points. They traded three punts with the Saints on subsequent drives before missing a field goal.

The Panthers were managed to convert a first down or touchdown on four of eleven attempts with four or fewer yards to gain. On those attempts, they called seven running and four passing plays. That's a better balance than I thought it was going to be after the game, but the truth remains simple: the Panthers need to find a way to consistently convert in these situations or they are going to have a disappointing end to an already middling season.

You're going to hear the word "discipline" this week as a critique of Dave Canales and his coaching staff about as often as you saw a flag thrown on the field yesterday. There were a couple of false starts and neutral zone infractions that were, indeed, characterized by a lack of discipline. However I would wager that the bulk of the 17 flags thrown for 152 penalty yards (only seventy fewer yards than the Saints total offensive output) were flags that most fans would describe as "bullshit."

When you lose and you point at one sided officiating, it's called being a sore loser. I guess the Carolina Panthers are in danger of making me a sore winner. Let's take a moment to consider the penalties that keyed the Saints three scoring drives.

1st & 10 at New Orleans 48, 8:46 3rd quarter, Panthers 7 Saints 0

Quarterback Tyler Shough found a wide open Jordan Tyson for an 18 yard pass. So far so clean. Tyson fell to the ground untouched while making the catch. Panthers linebacker Devin Lloyd fell onto and over Tyson in an effort to make sure he was downed by contact. This was flagged as unnecessary roughness.

The NFL rule book, under Rule 12. Section 2. Article 8 states that unnecessary roughness can be defined as the following:

running, diving into, or throwing the body against or on any player on the ground either before or after the ball is dead;

It's possible that Lloyd's momentum qualified under the letter of this law. It is also possible, to revive a quote from last week, that this could have been described as a "bang-bang play." Unfortunately, that term is not defined in the rule book.

The fifteen yards from that penalty, added to the reception, put the Saints into the red zone for the first time in the game. Unlike during the rest of the game, the Saints did proceed to score from there under their own power.

1st & 5 at Carolina 42, 6:18 4th quarter, Panthers 10 Saints 7

Jaelan Phillips, unheld, pursued Shough and brought him down for a 17-yard sack. This should have brought up second & 22 from the Saints' 39 yard line. Well out of field goal and certainly an uphill climb to get another first down.

Instead, a selective interpretation of Rule 12. Section 2. Article 16 was enforced, calling Phillips for a horse-collar tackle and giving the Saints 15 yards instead of them losing 17. The text of the rule is below with one part highlighted for emphasis.

No player shall grab the inside collar of the back or the side of the shoulder pads or jersey, or grab the jersey at the name plate or above, and pull the runner toward the ground. This does not apply to a runner who is in the pocket area or in the area defined by close-line play. If his knees are buckled by the action, it is a foul, even if the runner is not pulled completely to the ground.

Your next reasonable question is "how does the NFL define the pocket area?". Fortunately, that is easily answered by Rule 3. Section 25. "Pocket Area":

The pocket area is the area between the outside edges of the normal tackle positions on each side of the center extending backward to the offensive team's end line. A player who is in possession of the ball is out of the pocket area, and the pocket area no longer exists, if any part of his body or the ball is outside of the pocket area.

Between the inside hip of the right tackle and the center, 17 yards behind where the ball was snapped.

Is 17 yards behind the line of scrimmage outside of the pocket area?

No, the pocket area extends back to the offensive team's end line (the back of the end zone, in NFL parlance).

Were Shough's knees buckled by the action?

No, he fell to his side. He did not fall directly backwards in the way this rule was meant to penalize and prevent.

After this penalty, the Saints ran a total of four plays for an average of 1.2 yards per play. Unfortunately, a poorly timed neutral zone infraction by Trevon Moehrig (five-yard penalty) and a ticky-tack pass interference penalty on Corey Thornton (17-yard penalty, but I couldn't find a replay last night) added insult and further injury to the pile. Penalties, dubious penalties, accounted for 42 of the Saints 70 yards on this touchdown drive.

2nd & 7 at the New Orleans 37, 1:10 4th quarter, Panthers 17 Saints 14

Tyler Dodson tackled Jordyn Tyson high. His hand was clearly around the upper chest to neck area of Tyson and replay showed that not even a single finger contacted or snared Tyson's face mask. Nonetheless, Dodson was flagged for grabbing Tyson's facemask. We return to Rule 12. Section 2, this time Article 15 holds our answers:

If a player grasps an opponent's facemask or helmet opening, he must immediately release it. He shall not grasp and control, twist, turn, push, or pull the facemask or helmet opening of an opponent in any direction.

The only part of the rule that Dodson failed to comply with is that he did not immediately release Tyson's facemask. That is because he never grabbed it in the first place. He did not grasp and control, twist, turn, push, or pull the facemask or helmet opening of Tyson. So it must be Dodson's failure to release that which he did not ever grasp that gifted the Saints 15 yards on their tying field goal drive. Keep in mind that the Saints only advanced the ball six yards after this penalty before kicking their 55-yard field goal.

Yes, there are legitimate things to clean up, but they are not the bulk of yesterday's problem. If we want to talk about discipline, let's start with all 17 of the Saints points coming on drives that were extended or prosecuted in bulk by penalties that were at best questionable and often outright in contradiction to the rules. And that's just on defense. It doesn't account for the offensive pass interference call on Darren Waller or the holding call on Luke Fortner that couldn't have stood up to review. You can't cry discipline when the Panthers are being held to a separate and higher standard than their opponents.

You can, however, get excited for what Todd Wash is doing with his defense. These guys are playing short handed, being dealt short hands, and still came up with stops all day yesterday. Somehow, the defense is not the problem with the Carolina Panthers right now.

Questionable officiating and play calling doesn't excuse Bryce Young's performance yesterday. It was about as close to Year 1-3 Bryce as we have seen this season. Indecisive and inaccurate passing kept a couple of touchdowns off the board and led to his receivers hauling in only four of 12 targets on the day. I excused that kind of performance against the Tampa Bay Buccaneers in Week 7 and the Green Bay Packers in Week 8 because Young barely had time to catch the snap before he was under pressure.

The offensive line was far from perfect (looking at you, Walker), but they held up far better than expected against a mildly fearsome New Orleans front seven. They gave Young the kind of time that he feasted with in the opening quarter of the season and we all came home hungry for more yards through the air.

Good quarterbacks have bad games. Unfortunately for Young, it is starting to be too long since he has had a good game.

Hopefully a week off for Chuba Hubbard, to start. That man single-handedly made everything for the Panthers out of the many opportunities their defense afforded them. Give the man a break, a new contract, and just about anything else he asks for tomorrow.

Everybody should also be watching the Baltimore Raven take on the Los Angeles Chargers tomorrow night on Monday Night Football. The Ravens traded for Jacoby Brissett last week because both of their top two quarterbacks are currently on injured reserve and may be done for the season. Brissett is a 10-year veteran who is parachuting into a brand new situation in the middle of the season. Who knows how he'll gel with his new receivers, but we can get an inkling of what kind of challenge he'll pose when the Ravens visit the Panthers in just six days.

A questionable offense and an already struggling defense are just the kind of challenge that the Panthers feel up to right now. We'll see if those descriptions of the Ravens hold true tomorrow night.

#2810. Seahawks' team doctor OK after getting hit in the face by a Fernando Mendoza pass

Seahawks team physician Dr. Ed Khalfayan took a Fernando Mendoza fastball to the face on Sunday, but he emerged OK.

With the Raiders backed up deep in their own territory, Mendoza dropped back into his own end zone and fired a bullet toward the sideline. It sailed out of bounds near the 20-yard line, where Khalfayan was standing on the sideline. Khalfayan didn't see it coming, and it hit him hard in the head.

Khalfayan dropped to the ground and for a moment it appeared that he was knocked unconscious, although he was able to get back up quickly.

Reporters asked Seahawks coach Mike Macdonald after the game, and Macdonald said the sideline was alarmed by the sight of it.

"I was worried about him," Macdonald said. "That was scary, so we'll check on him. Thanks for asking."

Fortunately, Khalfayan told the Seahawks' website he was doing well after the game.

#2808. Mavericks score just 9 points in opening quarter during historic loss to Jayson Tatum, Celtics

The Dallas Mavericks got out to a horrific and historically bad start on Sunday night at TD Garden.

The Boston Celtics rolled to a 42-9 lead in the opening quarter. That marked Boston's largest lead at the end of the first period in franchise history.

It gets even worse for the Mavericks. The Celtics' 42-9 scoring margin was the best from any team in the league in any quarter since 1987, when the Los Angeles Lakers outscored the Sacramento Kings 40-4 in the first quarter, according to ESPN. It was also the third-best mark by a team in a quarter in the shot-clock era, which started back in 1954.

Remarkably, it wasn't the Mavericks' worst quarter in franchise history. They managed just two points in a quarter in a game against the Los Angeles Lakers in 1997.

On Sunday, the Mavericks made just two of their first 18 field-goal attempts in the opening quarter, and went a brutal 0 of 8 from behind the arc. Of their nine points in the opening frame, five of them came from the free-throw line.

"I didn't feel like we were turning it over early," Mavericks coach Dusty May said, via the Dallas Morning News' Mike Curtis. "I felt like we had some decent looks. We didn't convert them. I'd have to go back and look at the film for any specifics of why we weren't able to score."

And while things did get better for the Mavericks, it was far too late to make a difference. The Celtics took a 30-point lead into the locker room at halftime, and then rolled to the 131-94 victory.

Jayson Tatum led Boston with 32 points, 6 rebounds and 6 assists while shooting 11 of 18 from the field in the win. Derrick White added 16 points, and Baylor Scheierman finished with 12 points and five rebounds off the bench. Boston shot 51% from the field as a team and forced 17 turnovers.

The Celtics are now 11-2 on the season. They have won six straight, too, and hold a slight lead over the Philadelphia 76ers in the Eastern Conference standings.

Zaccharie Risacher led the Mavericks with 18 points off the bench after he went 8 of 9 from the field. Cooper Flagg finished with 17 points, and Walter Clayton added 13 points. The Mavericks were coming off a loss to the Cleveland Cavaliers in Ohio on Saturday night.

They have now lost three in a row and sit at 7-7 on the season. Thankfully, after a rough three-game road trip capped by a truly bad outing on Sunday, Flagg and the Mavericks will have three days to recover. They'll host the Golden State Warriors on Wednesday night.
